<a id='question-024'></a>

### Question 24: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 24 (aspect)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular defect and resulting immunological consequence in X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), specifically the failure of B-cell development due to a mutation in the Bruton tyrosine kinase (BTK) gene, leading to absent mature B cells and immunoglobulins. (考察X-连锁丙种球蛋白缺乏症（XLA）的分子缺陷和由此产生的免疫学后果，即由于布鲁顿酪氨酸激酶（BTK）基因突变导致B细胞发育失败，进而缺乏成熟B细胞和免疫球蛋白。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to his pediatrician with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including otitis media, sinusitis, and pneumonia, starting around 6 months of age after maternal antibodies waned. He has not received any live vaccines. Physical examination reveals normal growth and development, but no palpable lymph nodes or tonsils. Laboratory studies show markedly decreased serum levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE). Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals a severe deficiency of CD19+ B cells, while T-cell numbers (CD3+) and NK cell numbers (CD16/56+) are normal. Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *BTK* gene on the X chromosome.

Which of the following immunological defects is the underlying cause of this patient's condition?

(A) Defective T-cell receptor signaling leading to impaired T-cell activation and cytokine production.
(B) Impaired class switching recombination due to a deficiency in activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID).
(C) Failure of B-cell maturation beyond the pre-B cell stage due to a defect in intracellular signaling.
(D) Defective phagocyte chemotaxis and superoxide production due to a mutation in the NADPH oxidase complex.
(E) Impaired complement activation due to a deficiency in C3 convertase.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy. This age is typical for the onset of symptoms in XLA, as maternal IgG protection wanes around 6 months. Male sex points towards an X-linked disorder.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Recurrent sinopulmonary infections (otitis media, sinusitis, pneumonia) starting around 6 months. This pattern is characteristic of antibody deficiency, as encapsulated bacteria (like *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*) are major causes of these infections, and antibodies are crucial for opsonization and complement activation against them.
- **Physical Exam:** Absence of palpable lymph nodes and tonsils. B-cell follicles are normally found in these lymphoid tissues; their absence suggests a lack of B cells.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Markedly decreased serum levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE). This panhypogammaglobulinemia indicates a profound defect in antibody production.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Severe deficiency of CD19+ B cells, normal T-cell (CD3+) and NK cell (CD16/56+) numbers. CD19 is a pan-B cell marker. The absence of B cells confirms the primary defect lies within the B-cell lineage.
- **Genetic Testing:** Mutation in the *BTK* gene on the X chromosome. This confirms the diagnosis of X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying immunological defect.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **XLA Pathophysiology:** XLA is caused by mutations in the *BTK* gene, which encodes Bruton tyrosine kinase. BTK is a cytoplasmic tyrosine kinase essential for B-cell development.
- **B-cell Development:** B-cell development occurs primarily in the bone marrow. Pro-B cells progress to pre-B cells. The transition from pre-B cell to immature B cell involves rearrangement of the heavy chain locus and expression of IgM on the cell surface. Further maturation involves light chain rearrangement and expression of IgD. Mature, naive B cells then migrate to secondary lymphoid organs.
- **Role of BTK:** BTK is crucial for signaling downstream of the pre-B cell receptor (pre-BCR) and the B cell receptor (BCR). Signaling through these receptors is required for the survival, proliferation, and maturation of B cells beyond the pre-B cell stage. Specifically, BTK phosphorylates downstream targets like Syk, PLCγ2, and p85, initiating signaling cascades essential for B-cell development.
- **Consequence of BTK Deficiency:** In XLA, the lack of functional BTK prevents proper signaling through the pre-BCR and BCR. This leads to a block in B-cell development at the pre-B cell stage in the bone marrow. Consequently, mature B cells are absent in the peripheral blood and lymphoid tissues, leading to a lack of plasma cells and, therefore, a severe deficiency of all immunoglobulin isotypes (panhypogammaglobulinemia). T cells and NK cells develop normally because BTK is not required for their development.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective T-cell receptor signaling leading to impaired T-cell activation and cytokine production.** This describes defects in T-cell signaling pathways, such as those seen in certain forms of Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) or DiGeorge syndrome (if T-cell development is affected). The vignette clearly shows normal T-cell numbers (CD3+), ruling out a primary T-cell defect. The primary defect here is in B-cell development.
- (B) **Impaired class switching recombination due to a deficiency in activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID).** Deficiency in AID causes Hyper-IgM syndrome type 2. Patients have normal or elevated IgM levels but severely reduced IgG, IgA, and IgE. This is because AID is required for somatic hypermutation and class switch recombination (CSR) in B cells. While CSR is impaired in XLA B cells (as they don't mature), the primary defect is the *absence* of B cells, not just impaired CSR. Furthermore, IgM levels are also profoundly reduced in XLA, unlike Hyper-IgM syndrome.
- (D) **Defective phagocyte chemotaxis and superoxide production due to a mutation in the NADPH oxidase complex.** This describes Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). CGD affects phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages), leading to recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms (e.g., *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Aspergillus*). The vignette describes a B-cell defect (absent B cells, panhypogammaglobulinemia), not a phagocyte defect.
- (E) **Impaired complement activation due to a deficiency in C3 convertase.** Deficiencies in C3 convertase components (C3, C4, Factor B, Factor D) lead to impaired complement activation, increasing susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria. While complement deficiency can cause recurrent infections, the hallmark findings in this case (absent B cells, panhypogammaglobulinemia, *BTK* mutation) point specifically to XLA, a B-cell developmental defect, not a complement defect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) | Hyper-IgM Syndrome Type 2 (AID deficiency) | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | Common Variable Immunodeficiency (CVID) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | B-cell development (BTK mutation) | Class Switch Recombination (AID mutation) | Phagocyte function (NADPH oxidase)   | Variable B-cell/T-cell dysfunction    |
| **B-cell Numbers**      | Absent (Pre-B cell arrest)        | Normal/Increased                           | Normal                              | Normal/Decreased                       |
| **Ig Levels**           | All isotypes severely decreased   | IgM normal/high, IgG/IgA/IgE very low      | Normal                              | Variable, often low IgG, IgA, IgE      |
| **T-cell Numbers**      | Normal                            | Normal                                    | Normal                              | Normal                                 |
| **Key Infections**      | Encapsulated bacteria             | Encapsulated bacteria, opportunistic      | Catalase-positive organisms         | Encapsulated bacteria, viruses         |
| **Genetic Basis**       | X-linked (*BTK*)                  | Autosomal recessive (*AID*)                | X-linked or Autosomal recessive     | Variable                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
XLA is characterized by a block in B-cell maturation due to a *BTK* mutation, resulting in absent mature B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia. Key differentials include other primary antibody deficiencies like CVID (which typically presents later and has normal B cell numbers) and Hyper-IgM syndromes (which have normal B cell numbers but impaired class switching, often with normal/high IgM). Always correlate clinical presentation (recurrent bacterial infections), lab findings (low immunoglobulins, absent B cells), and genetic/molecular basis (*BTK* mutation) to arrive at the correct diagnosis.

<a id='question-025'></a>

### Question 25: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 25 (immunity,)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on the role of complement components (C3, C5) in opsonization and the resulting susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria in patients with complement deficiencies. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是补体成分（C3，C5）在吞噬作用中的作用，以及补体缺乏患者对荚膜细菌的易感性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a high fever, lethargy, and signs of sepsis. He has a history of recurrent, severe bacterial infections, including pneumonia, meningitis, and otitis media, starting in infancy. His mother reports that he often has difficulty clearing common childhood infections. Physical examination reveals a petechial rash and signs of meningeal irritation. Laboratory studies show markedly decreased serum levels of C3 and C5, while C1q, C2, C4, and C6-C9 levels are normal. Blood cultures grow *Neisseria meningitidis*.

Which of the following mechanisms best explains this patient's increased susceptibility to infection with *Neisseria meningitidis*?

(A) Impaired antibody production against polysaccharide capsules due to defective B cell development.
(B) Defective phagocytosis of opsonized bacteria due to the absence of functional complement receptors (CR1) on phagocytes.
(C) Inability to form the membrane attack complex (MAC) leading to impaired killing of intracellular bacteria.
(D) Reduced production of cytokines, such as IL-12, necessary for effective T cell activation and macrophage function.
(E) Defective T cell-dependent B cell activation, resulting in a lack of class switching and isotype switching.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy with recurrent, severe bacterial infections (pneumonia, meningitis, otitis media) since infancy. This points towards a primary immunodeficiency affecting bacterial clearance.
- **Specific Infection:** *Neisseria meningitidis* meningitis. *N. meningitidis* is an encapsulated bacterium.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Markedly decreased serum C3 and C5 levels, with normal levels of early (C1q, C2, C4) and late (C6-C9) complement components. This pattern is characteristic of a deficiency in the alternative pathway or a defect in the common pathway (C5 convertase). Since C3 and C5 are low, the common pathway (C5 convertase) is impaired, leading to reduced C5 cleavage and subsequent C5b-C9 (MAC) formation, and also reduced C3 cleavage leading to reduced C3b formation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining susceptibility to *N. meningitidis*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Complement System Overview:** The complement system is crucial for innate immunity. It has three pathways (classical, alternative, lectin) converging on C3 convertase (C4b2a or C3bBb) and C5 convertase (C4b2a3b or C3bBb3b). C3 convertase cleaves C3 into C3a (anaphylatoxin) and C3b (opsonin). C5 convertase cleaves C5 into C5a (anaphylatoxin, chemotactic factor) and C5b, which initiates the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC, C5b-9).
- **Opsonization:** C3b is a major opsonin. It coats pathogens, facilitating their recognition and phagocytosis by cells expressing complement receptors, primarily CR1 (CD35), which binds C3b.
- **Patient's Defect:** Low C3 and C5 indicate a defect in the common pathway (C5 convertase activity) or the alternative pathway (which generates C3bBb). The specific pattern (low C3, low C5, normal early components) strongly suggests a deficiency in C5 or a defect in C5 convertase activity.
- **Mechanism of Susceptibility:** Deficiency in C3 or C5 leads to:
    1.  **Reduced Opsonization:** Less C3b is generated, impairing the opsonization of encapsulated bacteria like *N. meningitidis*. Phagocytes cannot efficiently bind and engulf these bacteria via CR1.
    2.  **Impaired MAC Formation:** Less C5b is generated, leading to reduced formation of the MAC, which is important for lysing certain bacteria, especially Gram-negative bacteria like *N. meningitidis*.
- **Connecting to the Question:** The patient's low C3 and C5 levels impair opsonization (reduced C3b) and MAC formation (reduced C5b). Option (B) directly addresses the impaired phagocytosis of opsonized bacteria due to the lack of functional complement receptors (CR1) binding to reduced C3b. This is a direct consequence of the complement deficiency.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Impaired antibody production against polysaccharide capsules due to defective B cell development. This describes conditions like X-linked agammaglobulinemia (Bruton's) or Common Variable Immunodeficiency (CVID). While these cause susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria, the patient's complement levels (C3, C5) are specifically low, not indicative of a primary B cell defect. Antibody production is typically normal in complement deficiencies.
- (C) Inability to form the membrane attack complex (MAC) leading to impaired killing of intracellular bacteria. While MAC formation is impaired due to low C5, the primary susceptibility in C3/C5 deficiency is to *extracellular* encapsulated bacteria, not intracellular bacteria. Impaired MAC formation contributes to susceptibility, but the *major* defect leading to susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria is impaired opsonization (C3b). Also, phagocytosis is the primary mechanism for clearing encapsulated bacteria, not intracellular killing by MAC.
- (D) Reduced production of cytokines, such as IL-12, necessary for effective T cell activation and macrophage function. This describes defects in T cell function or cytokine signaling pathways (e.g., STAT1 deficiency). The patient's presentation and lab findings (low C3/C5) do not point to a T cell or cytokine defect.
- (E) Defective T cell-dependent B cell activation, resulting in a lack of class switching and isotype switching. This describes conditions like Hyper-IgM syndrome (CD40L deficiency or CD40 deficiency). Patients with Hyper-IgM syndrome have normal or high IgM but low IgG, IgA, IgE, and are susceptible to opportunistic infections. The patient's complement levels are normal in Hyper-IgM syndrome, and the specific low C3/C5 pattern is absent.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | C3/C5 Deficiency (This Case) | Antibody Deficiency (e.g., XLA) | MAC Deficiency (C5-C9) | T Cell Deficiency (e.g., DiGeorge) | Hyper-IgM Syndrome |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :-------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :----------------- |
| **Key Defect**          | Low C3/C5                   | Low/Absent Antibodies (IgG, IgA) | Defective C5-C9       | Defective T Cells                 | Defective Class Switch |
| **Complement Levels**   | Low C3, Low C5              | Normal                         | Low C5-C9             | Normal                            | Normal             |
| **Primary Susceptibility** | Encapsulated Bacteria       | Encapsulated Bacteria          | Neisseria spp.        | Viral, Fungal, Protozoal          | Opportunistic Infections |
| **Mechanism**           | Impaired Opsonization (C3b) & MAC | Impaired Opsonization & Neutralization | Impaired Lysis       | Impaired Cell-Mediated Immunity | Impaired Isotype Switch |
| **Phagocytosis**        | Impaired (CR1 binding C3b)  | Impaired (CR1 binding Ab/C3b) | Relatively Normal     | Normal                            | Normal             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Complement deficiencies, particularly those affecting C3 or C5, lead to impaired opsonization (reduced C3b) and reduced MAC formation. The primary clinical consequence is increased susceptibility to infections with encapsulated bacteria like *Neisseria meningitidis*, *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, and *Haemophilus influenzae*. The impaired opsonization directly affects phagocytosis via CR1 receptors. (补体缺乏，特别是影响C3或C5的缺乏，导致吞噬作用受损（C3b减少）和MAC形成减少。主要临床后果是对荚膜细菌，如脑膜炎奈瑟菌、肺炎链球菌和流感嗜血杆菌的感染易感性增加。受损的吞噬作用直接影响通过CR1受体介导的吞噬作用。) Differentiate C3/C5 deficiency from other primary immunodeficiencies based on specific complement component levels and clinical presentation.

<a id='question-026'></a>

### Question 26: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 26 (including)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in complement pathways and their clinical manifestations, particularly susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria and autoimmune phenomena. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是补体通路的缺陷及其临床表现，例如对荚膜细菌的易感性和自身免疫现象。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with recurrent episodes of bacterial pneumonia, meningitis, and sepsis, primarily caused by *Neisseria meningitidis* and *Streptococcus pneumoniae*. He also has a history of recurrent skin infections with *Staphylococcus aureus*. Family history reveals that his maternal grandfather died young from a suspected autoimmune disease involving glomerulonephritis. Laboratory investigations show normal immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM) and normal T-cell counts and function. However, flow cytometry reveals a significant deficiency of CD59 expression on neutrophils and monocytes. Serum complement levels (C3, C4) are normal, but the CH50 (total hemolytic complement activity) is markedly reduced. A specific assay for the alternative pathway shows normal C3 convertase activity but severely impaired C5 convertase activity.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying defect in this patient?

(A) Deficiency of C1q, C1r, or C1s components
(B) Deficiency of Factor D
(C) Deficiency of Properdin (Factor P)
(D) Deficiency of Membrane cofactor protein (MCP, CD46)
(E) Deficiency of C5-C9 components (Membrane Attack Complex, MAC)

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria (*N. meningitidis*, *S. pneumoniae*) and *S. aureus***: This points towards a defect in opsonization or complement-mediated killing, as these organisms are typically cleared by phagocytosis enhanced by complement (C3b) or direct lysis by the Membrane Attack Complex (MAC).
*   **Normal immunoglobulin levels and T-cell function**: This rules out common variable immunodeficiency (CVID), X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID), and other primary antibody or T-cell defects.
*   **Normal C3, C4 levels, but low CH50**: This indicates that the classical and lectin pathways (which generate C3 and C4) are functioning adequately, but the terminal pathway (C5-C9) or a pathway leading to C5 activation is impaired.
*   **Deficiency of CD59 on neutrophils/monocytes**: CD59 (Protectin) is a membrane-bound complement regulatory protein that inhibits the formation of the MAC on host cells. Its deficiency leads to increased susceptibility to MAC-mediated lysis, particularly in the context of autoimmune diseases. However, the primary defect here is likely related to the *formation* of the MAC, not its regulation on host cells. The CD59 deficiency might be a secondary finding or related to the underlying complement pathway defect, but it doesn't explain the susceptibility to bacterial infections.
*   **Normal alternative pathway C3 convertase activity, impaired C5 convertase activity**: The alternative pathway C3 convertase (C3bBb) is functional (normal C3 levels), but the C5 convertase (C3bBbC3b) is impaired. This strongly suggests a defect in a component required for C5 convertase formation or function *after* C3bBb is formed.
*   **Family history of autoimmune glomerulonephritis**: This is a common feature of complement deficiencies, particularly those affecting the alternative pathway, due to impaired clearance of immune complexes and apoptotic cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Complement System Overview**: The complement system is crucial for innate immunity, opsonization, inflammation, and lysis of pathogens. It has three activation pathways: classical (antibody-dependent), lectin (mannose-binding lectin-dependent), and alternative (spontaneous activation). All converge on C3 convertase (C3bBb or C4b2a), which cleaves C3 into C3a and C3b. C3b is a potent opsonin and part of the C5 convertase. The C5 convertase (C4b2a3b or C3bBbC3b) cleaves C5 into C5a and C5b. C5b initiates the formation of the Membrane Attack Complex (MAC, C5b-9), which lyses pathogens.
*   **Alternative Pathway**: This pathway is continuously active at low levels and is amplified by microbial surfaces. It involves spontaneous hydrolysis of C3 to C3(H2O), which binds Factor B. Factor D cleaves Factor B to Ba and Bb. C3(H2O)Bb is the initial C3 convertase. It cleaves C3 into C3a and C3b. C3b binds to the surface and associates with the C3 convertase to form C3bBbC3b, the C5 convertase. Properdin (Factor P) stabilizes this C5 convertase, significantly amplifying the pathway.
*   **Deficiency of Properdin (Factor P)**: Properdin is essential for stabilizing the alternative pathway C5 convertase (C3bBbC3b) on microbial surfaces. Deficiency leads to impaired formation of the MAC, resulting in susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria (*N. meningitidis*, *S. pneumoniae*, *H. influenzae*) and *Neisseria gonorrhoeae*. It also impairs the clearance of immune complexes, leading to increased risk of autoimmune diseases like glomerulonephritis, SLE, and recurrent pregnancy loss. The clinical picture (recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria, autoimmune history, normal C3/C4 but low CH50, impaired C5 convertase activity) perfectly matches Factor P deficiency. The normal C3 convertase activity (C3bBb) indicates the initial steps of the alternative pathway are intact, but the stabilization and subsequent C5 convertase formation are deficient due to lack of Properdin.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Deficiency of C1q, C1r, or C1s components**: These components are part of the classical pathway. Deficiency leads to impaired activation of the classical pathway, resulting in low C3 and C4 levels, increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria, and increased risk of SLE. This patient has normal C3 and C4 levels, making this option incorrect.
*   **(B) Deficiency of Factor D**: Factor D is a serine protease that cleaves Factor B in the alternative pathway. Deficiency impairs the formation of the alternative pathway C3 convertase (C3bBb) and C5 convertase. This would lead to low C3 and C4 levels (due to lack of amplification) and susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria. However, this patient has normal C3 and C4 levels and normal C3 convertase activity, making this option incorrect.
*   **(D) Deficiency of Membrane cofactor protein (MCP, CD46)**: MCP is a complement regulatory protein found on host cells. It acts as a cofactor for Factor I-mediated cleavage of C3b and C4b, preventing excessive complement activation on host tissues. Deficiency leads to uncontrolled complement activation, resulting in PNH (Paroxysmal Nocturnal Hemoglobinuria) due to intravascular hemolysis and thrombosis. It does not typically cause susceptibility to bacterial infections or autoimmune disease in the way described.
*   **(E) Deficiency of C5-C9 components (Membrane Attack Complex, MAC)**: Deficiency of any component of the MAC (C5, C6, C7, C8, C9) impairs the formation of the terminal complex, leading to susceptibility to *Neisseria* species (especially *N. meningitidis* and *N. gonorrhoeae*), which are particularly sensitive to MAC-mediated lysis. While this patient is susceptible to *N. meningitidis*, the specific finding of impaired C5 convertase activity points more strongly towards a defect *upstream* of C5, specifically in the stabilization of the C5 convertase in the alternative pathway. Also, MAC deficiency typically presents with normal CH50 if the classical/lectin pathways are intact and can activate C5, although CH50 can be low. The key differentiator here is the specific defect in C5 convertase activity in the alternative pathway, strongly implicating Properdin.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | Properdin (Factor P) Deficiency | C1q/r/s Deficiency | Factor D Deficiency | MCP (CD46) Deficiency | C5-C9 Deficiency |
| :-------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :----------------- | :------------------ | :-------------------- | :--------------- |
| Pathway Affected            | Alternative                    | Classical          | Alternative         | Regulation            | Terminal         |
| C3/C4 Levels                | Normal                         | Low                | Low                 | Normal                | Normal           |
| CH50                        | Low                            | Low                | Low                 | Normal                | Low              |
| Susceptibility              | Encapsulated bacteria, *Neisseria* | Encapsulated bacteria | Encapsulated bacteria | PNH, Thrombosis       | *Neisseria*      |
| Autoimmunity                | Yes (Glomerulonephritis, SLE)   | Yes (SLE)          | Less common         | No                    | No               |
| C5 Convertase Activity      | Impaired                       | Normal             | Impaired            | Normal                | Normal           |
| CD59 Expression             | Normal                         | Normal             | Normal              | Normal                | Normal           |
| Key Defect                  | C5 convertase stabilization    | Classical initiation | C3 convertase formation | Host cell regulation | MAC formation    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria, coupled with normal early complement components (C3, C4) but low CH50 and impaired alternative pathway C5 convertase activity, is characteristic of Properdin (Factor P) deficiency. Remember to differentiate this from other complement deficiencies based on affected pathway, C3/C4 levels, CH50, and specific clinical features like PNH (MCP deficiency) or susceptibility primarily to *Neisseria* (MAC deficiency).

<a id='question-027'></a>

### Question 27: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 27 (innate)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Defective Toll-like receptor (TLR) signaling impairs innate immune recognition of microbial components, leading to increased susceptibility to specific pathogens. (Toll 样受体信号通路缺陷导致先天免疫对微生物成分的识别受损，从而增加对特定病原体的易感性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of high fever, lethargy, and a diffuse maculopapular rash. He was born full-term without complications but has had recurrent episodes of pneumonia (Streptococcus pneumoniae, Haemophilus influenzae) and otitis media since infancy. His mother reports that he also had a severe, non-healing skin abscess after a minor cut last year, which required multiple debridements and cultures grew *Staphylococcus aureus*. Physical examination reveals a temperature of 39.5°C, a generalized erythematous maculopapular rash, and hepatosplenomegaly. Laboratory studies show leukocytosis with neutrophilia. A skin biopsy from the rash shows a dense infiltrate of neutrophils, but these neutrophils exhibit impaired chemotaxis towards a bacterial peptide (fMLP) in vitro. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *TLR4* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely mechanism underlying this patient's recurrent infections and impaired neutrophil function?

(A) Defective phagosome-lysosome fusion due to impaired actin polymerization.
(B) Impaired production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) due to defective NADPH oxidase.
(C) Defective recognition of bacterial lipopolysaccharide (LPS) leading to inadequate activation of the innate immune response.
(D) Defective complement component C3 production leading to impaired opsonization and bacterial killing.
(E) Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling leading to impaired adaptive immune responses.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy with recurrent infections (pneumonia, otitis media, skin abscess) since infancy. This points towards a primary immunodeficiency.
- **Infections:** *S. pneumoniae*, *H. influenzae*, *S. aureus*. These are common encapsulated bacteria and pyogenic bacteria, often targeted by innate immunity (phagocytes, complement) and adaptive immunity (antibodies). Recurrent infections with these suggest a defect in early immune responses.
- **Skin Abscess:** Severe, non-healing *S. aureus* abscess suggests a defect in neutrophil function or chemotaxis.
- **Physical Exam:** Fever, rash, hepatosplenomegaly. Rash could be related to infection or inflammation. Hepatosplenomegaly can be seen in chronic infections or inflammatory states.
- **Neutrophil Function:** Impaired chemotaxis towards fMLP (a bacterial peptide) in vitro. This specifically points to a defect in neutrophil migration or activation.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the *TLR4* gene. TLR4 is a key pattern recognition receptor (PRR) involved in recognizing bacterial lipopolysaccharide (LPS), a component of the outer membrane of Gram-negative bacteria.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism underlying the recurrent infections and impaired neutrophil function, given the *TLR4* mutation.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **TLR4 Function:** TLR4, along with MD-2 and CD14, recognizes LPS (endotoxin) from Gram-negative bacteria. Upon LPS binding, TLR4 initiates intracellular signaling cascades involving MyD88 and TRIF adaptors, leading to the activation of transcription factors like NF-κB and IRFs.
- **Downstream Effects of TLR4 Activation:** Activation of NF-κB leads to the production of pro-inflammatory cytokines (e.g., TNF-α, IL-1β, IL-6) and chemokines (e.g., IL-8), which are crucial for recruiting and activating immune cells, including neutrophils, to the site of infection. TLR4 signaling is essential for the innate immune response against Gram-negative bacteria and also contributes to responses against other pathogens.
- **TLR4 Deficiency:** A mutation in *TLR4* (as in this patient) impairs the recognition of LPS. This leads to:
    - **Reduced Cytokine Production:** Decreased production of pro-inflammatory cytokines and chemokines upon exposure to LPS-containing bacteria.
    - **Impaired Neutrophil Activation/Chemotaxis:** Neutrophils may not be adequately activated or recruited to sites of infection, explaining the impaired chemotaxis observed in vitro.
    - **Increased Susceptibility:** Increased susceptibility to infections, particularly by Gram-negative bacteria (though susceptibility to pyogenic bacteria like *S. aureus* can also be increased due to impaired overall innate response).
- **Connecting to the Case:** The patient's recurrent infections with common bacterial pathogens (*S. pneumoniae*, *H. influenzae*, *S. aureus*) and the specific finding of impaired neutrophil chemotaxis, coupled with the *TLR4* mutation, strongly indicate that the underlying mechanism is defective recognition of bacterial components (specifically LPS) leading to inadequate innate immune activation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective phagosome-lysosome fusion due to impaired actin polymerization.** This describes Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD), caused by defects in NADPH oxidase subunits (e.g., gp91phox, p47phox). CGD leads to impaired killing of phagocytosed microbes, particularly catalase-positive organisms like *S. aureus*, *Aspergillus*, *Serratia*. While the patient has *S. aureus* infections, the primary defect here is TLR4 signaling, not phagosome-lysosome fusion. Actin polymerization is involved in phagocytosis and chemotaxis, but the specific defect in this case is TLR4-mediated signaling, not actin itself.
- (B) **Impaired production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) due to defective NADPH oxidase.** This is the hallmark of Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD), as explained above. The patient's defect is in TLR4 signaling, not ROS production.
- (D) **Defective complement component C3 production leading to impaired opsonization and bacterial killing.** Defective C3 production is characteristic of certain complement deficiencies (e.g., C3 deficiency). While complement is crucial for opsonization and killing, the primary defect identified in this patient is *TLR4*, which is part of the innate immune system but distinct from the complement cascade. TLR4 signaling can influence complement activation, but the core problem here is the lack of TLR4 response.
- (E) **Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling leading to impaired adaptive immune responses.** This describes defects in T-cell development or function, such as DiGeorge syndrome (thymic hypoplasia) or severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) affecting T-cell maturation. While adaptive immunity is important, the patient's presentation (early onset, recurrent bacterial infections, impaired neutrophil chemotaxis) and the specific *TLR4* mutation point strongly towards a primary defect in innate immunity, not T-cell signaling.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | TLR4 Deficiency (This Case) | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | Complement Deficiency (e.g., C3) | T-cell Deficiency (e.g., SCID) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :----------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | TLR4 Signaling              | NADPH Oxidase                       | Complement Component (e.g., C3)   | T-cell Receptor/Development    |
| **Key Pathogens**       | Gram-negative bacteria, pyogenic bacteria | Catalase-positive organisms (*S. aureus*, *Aspergillus*) | Encapsulated bacteria (*S. pneumoniae*, *H. influenzae*) | Opportunistic infections (viral, fungal, bacterial) |
| **Neutrophil Function** | Impaired chemotaxis/activation | Impaired killing (ROS production)   | Normal (usually)                  | Normal (usually)               |
| **Genetic Basis**       | *TLR4* mutation              | *CYBB*, *NCF1*, *NCF2*, *NCF4* mutations | Various genes (*C3*, etc.)        | Various genes (*IL2RG*, etc.)  |
| **Clinical Manifestations** | Recurrent bacterial infections, sepsis | Granulomas, abscesses, recurrent infections | Recurrent encapsulated bacterial infections | Severe, recurrent infections early in life |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Defects in Toll-like receptors (TLRs), key components of the innate immune system, impair the recognition of pathogen-associated molecular patterns (PAMPs) like LPS, leading to inadequate inflammatory responses and increased susceptibility to specific infections. Remember to differentiate TLR4 deficiency from other primary immunodeficiencies affecting neutrophil function (CGD) or opsonization (complement deficiency).

<a id='question-028'></a>

### Question 28: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 28 (adaptive)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies affecting adaptive immunity, specifically focusing on defects in T-cell development and function, leading to susceptibility to specific types of infections. The key is recognizing the clinical presentation and immunological findings consistent with DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome) and its impact on T-cell maturation. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是影响适应性免疫的缺陷，重点是T细胞发育和功能障碍，导致对特定类型感染的易感性。关键在于识别与DiGeorge综合征（22q11.2缺失综合征）一致的临床表现和免疫学发现，及其对T细胞成熟的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant presents to the pediatric emergency department with recurrent episodes of thrush (oral candidiasis) and failure to thrive. His mother reports that he has had persistent skin rashes, particularly in the diaper area, and several episodes of pneumonia requiring hospitalization. Physical examination reveals a small, somewhat dysmorphic face with hypertelorism, a low-set mouth, and a prominent nasal bridge. Cardiac auscultation reveals a murmur consistent with conotruncal heart defects. Laboratory investigations show lymphopenia (absolute lymphocyte count 500/µL, normal range 1500-7000/µL) with markedly reduced T-cell numbers (CD3+ T cells 100/µL, normal range 500-1500/µL) and normal B-cell numbers (CD19+ B cells 500/µL, normal range 700-2000/µL). Immunoglobulin levels are low (IgG 200 mg/dL, normal range 700-1500 mg/dL; IgM 20 mg/dL, normal range 40-200 mg/dL; IgA <5 mg/dL, normal range 70-400 mg/dL). Flow cytometry confirms the absence of a distinct T-cell subset. Karyotyping reveals a microdeletion on chromosome 22q11.2. Which of the following immunological defects is most likely responsible for this patient's recurrent infections?

(A) Defective B-cell maturation leading to impaired antibody production against encapsulated bacteria.
(B) Impaired phagocyte function resulting in increased susceptibility to catalase-positive organisms.
(C) Deficient T-cell development due to abnormal thymic development.
(D) Defective complement activation leading to increased risk of Neisseria infections.
(E) Impaired cytokine signaling causing defective macrophage activation and granuloma formation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 6-month-old male infant. Age is critical as many primary immunodeficiencies manifest in infancy.
*   **Presenting Symptoms:** Recurrent thrush (Candida albicans), failure to thrive, persistent skin rashes (likely fungal or viral), recurrent pneumonia. These suggest a defect in cell-mediated immunity (T-cells) and/or humoral immunity (B-cells/antibodies). Thrush and skin rashes point towards fungal/viral issues often controlled by T-cells. Pneumonia suggests bacterial or viral susceptibility.
*   **Physical Exam:** Dysmorphic features (hypertelorism, low-set mouth, prominent nasal bridge), conotruncal heart defects. These are characteristic features associated with DiGeorge syndrome.
*   **Laboratory Findings:**
    *   Lymphopenia: Low total lymphocyte count.
    *   Markedly reduced T-cells (CD3+): Specific deficiency in T-lymphocytes.
    *   Normal B-cells (CD19+): B-lymphocyte numbers are relatively preserved.
    *   Low Immunoglobulins (IgG, IgM, IgA): Hypogammaglobulinemia, likely secondary to the lack of T-cell help for B-cell maturation and antibody production.
    *   Flow cytometry: Absence of a distinct T-cell subset confirms the T-cell deficiency.
    *   Karyotyping: 22q11.2 microdeletion confirms DiGeorge syndrome.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most likely* immunological defect responsible for the recurrent infections.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 Deletion Syndrome):** Caused by a microdeletion on chromosome 22q11.2. This region contains the *TBX1* gene, crucial for the development of the 3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouches.
*   **Pharyngeal Pouches & Thymus:** The 3rd pharyngeal pouch gives rise to the thymus, and the 4th pharyngeal pouch contributes to the parathyroid glands.
*   **Thymic Hypoplasia/Aplasia:** In DiGeorge syndrome, abnormal development of the 3rd pharyngeal pouch leads to thymic hypoplasia or aplasia. The thymus is the primary site for T-cell maturation.
*   **T-cell Deficiency:** Lack of a functional thymus results in severely impaired T-cell development and maturation. This leads to a profound deficiency in circulating T-lymphocytes (CD3+ cells, including CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells).
*   **Consequences of T-cell Deficiency:**
    *   Impaired cell-mediated immunity: Increased susceptibility to intracellular pathogens (viruses, fungi like Candida, intracellular bacteria like Pneumocystis jirovecii).
    *   Impaired humoral immunity: T-helper cells (CD4+) are essential for B-cell activation, class switching, and memory B-cell formation. T-cell deficiency leads to secondary hypogammaglobulinemia (low IgG, IgM, IgA) despite normal or near-normal B-cell numbers.
    *   Clinical Manifestations: Recurrent infections (thrush, pneumonia, skin rashes), failure to thrive.
*   **Other Features of DiGeorge Syndrome:** Cardiac defects (conotruncal anomalies like tetralogy of Fallot, interrupted aortic arch), hypoparathyroidism (leading to hypocalcemia), characteristic facial features.

*   **Option (C) Analysis:** Deficient T-cell development due to abnormal thymic development directly explains the lymphopenia, low CD3+ T-cell count, low immunoglobulin levels (due to lack of T-cell help), and susceptibility to opportunistic infections like Candida and Pneumocystis (implied by recurrent pneumonia). This aligns perfectly with the clinical and laboratory findings in DiGeorge syndrome.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Defective B-cell maturation leading to impaired antibody production against encapsulated bacteria:** This describes defects like X-linked agammaglobulinemia (Bruton's) or Common Variable Immunodeficiency (CVID). While the patient has low immunoglobulins and recurrent infections, the *primary* defect is T-cell development (thymus), not B-cell maturation. B-cell numbers are normal, indicating the B-cells are present but not functioning properly due to lack of T-cell help. This option misidentifies the primary defect.
*   **(B) Impaired phagocyte function resulting in increased susceptibility to catalase-positive organisms:** This describes Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). CGD patients have recurrent bacterial and fungal infections (especially catalase-positive organisms like *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Aspergillus*), but their lymphocyte counts (T and B cells) and immunoglobulin levels are typically normal. The vignette clearly points to a T-cell defect.
*   **(D) Defective complement activation leading to increased risk of Neisseria infections:** This describes deficiencies in the terminal complement pathway (C5-C9) or early components (C1, C2, C4). Patients are susceptible to encapsulated bacteria (like *Neisseria meningitidis*, *Neisseria gonorrhoeae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*) but typically have normal lymphocyte counts and immunoglobulin levels. The vignette's findings are inconsistent with a primary complement defect.
*   **(E) Impaired cytokine signaling causing defective macrophage activation and granuloma formation:** This describes defects in cytokine receptors or signaling pathways, such as STAT1 deficiency or IFN-gamma receptor deficiency. These conditions lead to susceptibility to intracellular pathogens (like mycobacteria, Salmonella) and impaired granuloma formation. While T-cells produce cytokines, the primary defect in this vignette is T-cell *development* due to thymic issues, not a downstream cytokine signaling problem. The specific constellation of findings (thymic aplasia, T-cell deficiency, cardiac defects, facial features) strongly points to DiGeorge syndrome.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 deletion) | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | Complement Deficiency (e.g., C5-C9) | STAT1 Deficiency |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :-------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | T-cell development (thymic aplasia)  | B-cell maturation                 | Phagocyte oxidative burst            | Complement activation                | Cytokine signaling |
| **Cell Type Affected**  | T-cells (CD3+)                       | B-cells (CD19+), Plasma cells      | Neutrophils, Macrophages             | N/A                                  | Macrophages, T-cells |
| **Lymphocyte Count**    | Low T-cells, Normal/Low B-cells      | Normal T-cells, Very Low B-cells  | Normal                                | Normal                                | Normal          |
| **Immunoglobulins**     | Low (secondary to T-cell defect)     | Very Low (all classes)            | Normal                                | Normal                                | Normal          |
| **Key Infections**      | Candida, Pneumocystis, Viruses       | Encapsulated bacteria (Pyogenic)  | Catalase+ organisms (Staph, Asp)     | Neisseria, Encapsulated bacteria    | Mycobacteria, Salmonella |
| **Other Features**      | Cardiac defects, Hypocalcemia, Facial dysmorphism | N/A                               | Granulomas                           | N/A                                  | Autoimmunity    |
| **Pathophysiology**     | *TBX1* gene deletion -> Thymic hypoplasia | *BTK* gene mutation -> Pre-B cell arrest | *CYBB* (or other NADPH oxidase subunit) mutation -> Defective ROS | Gene mutations in complement components | *STAT1* mutation -> Impaired IFN signaling |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion) is a primary immunodeficiency characterized by thymic hypoplasia/aplasia leading to severe T-cell deficiency. This results in susceptibility to opportunistic infections (fungal, viral, Pneumocystis) and secondary hypogammaglobulinemia due to lack of T-cell help. Key clinical features include cardiac defects, hypocalcemia, and characteristic facial features. Differentiate from other primary immunodeficiencies based on the specific cell type affected (T-cell, B-cell, phagocyte, complement) and the resulting clinical manifestations.

<a id='question-029'></a>

### Question 029: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 29 (branches.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies affecting lymphocyte development and maturation, specifically focusing on defects in lymphocyte receptor signaling pathways and their clinical consequences, particularly susceptibility to specific types of infections. (考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是淋巴细胞受体信号通路缺陷及其临床后果，如对特定类型感染的易感性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, cough, and shortness of breath. He has a history of recurrent sinopulmonary infections since infancy, including pneumonia requiring hospitalization twice. His mother reports that he has also experienced chronic diarrhea and failure to thrive. Physical examination reveals a cachectic child with bilateral crackles in the lungs. Laboratory studies show lymphopenia (absolute lymphocyte count 500/µL), markedly reduced levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM), and normal complement levels (C3, C4). Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals a lack of B cells (CD19+) and T cells (CD3+). Further investigation reveals that the patient's T cells, when stimulated with anti-CD3/anti-CD28 antibodies, fail to proliferate or produce cytokines like IL-2. His B cells, when stimulated with anti-IgM antibodies, also fail to proliferate or differentiate into plasma cells. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *CD3δ* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological defect in this patient?

(A) Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to a mutation in the *ZAP70* gene.
(B) Impaired B-cell development due to a mutation in the *BTK* gene.
(C) Defective immunoglobulin class switching due to a mutation in the *AID* gene.
(D) Impaired T-cell and B-cell receptor (TCR/BCR) signaling due to a mutation in the *CD3δ* gene.
(E) Defective B-cell maturation due to a mutation in the *μ* heavy chain gene.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 6-year-old boy with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, chronic diarrhea, failure to thrive, lymphopenia, panhypogammaglobulinemia (low IgG, IgA, IgM), normal complement. This clinical picture strongly suggests a severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or a severe primary immunodeficiency affecting both T and B cell function.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Lack of both B cells (CD19+) and T cells (CD3+) indicates a profound defect in lymphocyte development or survival.
- **Functional Assays:** T cells fail to proliferate or produce IL-2 upon TCR stimulation (anti-CD3/anti-CD28). B cells fail to proliferate or differentiate upon BCR stimulation (anti-IgM). This points to a defect in the signaling pathways downstream of both TCR and BCR.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the *CD3δ* gene. The CD3 complex (composed of γ, δ, ε, and ζ chains) is essential for TCR signaling. The δ chain is crucial for associating the TCR with the ζ chain, which contains the ITAMs (Immunoreceptor Tyrosine-based Activation Motifs) necessary for signal transduction.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying immunological defect.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **TCR/BCR Signaling:** Both T-cell receptors (TCRs) and B-cell receptors (BCRs) are transmembrane receptors that initiate signaling cascades upon ligand binding. The TCR complex includes the TCRαβ or γδ heterodimer and the CD3 complex (γ, δ, ε, ζ chains). The BCR complex includes the membrane-bound immunoglobulin (Igαβ) and the Igα/Igβ heterodimer.
- **CD3 Complex Role:** The CD3 complex is non-covalently linked to the TCR and is essential for transducing the signal from the TCR to the intracellular signaling machinery. The ζ chain within the CD3 complex contains ITAMs. Upon TCR engagement, Src family kinases (like Lck) phosphorylate the ITAMs on the ζ chain. This recruits and activates ZAP70 (Zeta-chain-associated protein kinase 70), which then phosphorylates downstream targets like LAT (Linker for Activation of T cells), leading to the activation of various signaling pathways (PLCγ, Ras/MAPK, NF-κB, NFAT) that result in T-cell activation, proliferation, cytokine production, and effector functions.
- **BCR Signaling:** The Igα/Igβ heterodimer contains ITAMs. Upon BCR engagement, Src family kinases phosphorylate these ITAMs, recruiting Syk (Spleen tyrosine kinase), which then phosphorylates downstream targets like BLNK (B-cell linker), leading to B-cell activation, proliferation, and differentiation.
- **CD3δ Mutation:** A mutation in the *CD3δ* gene disrupts the assembly or function of the CD3 complex. This prevents proper TCR signaling, leading to impaired T-cell development (thymic selection defects) and function (failure to proliferate or produce cytokines). Since the CD3 complex is essential for TCR signaling, and TCR signaling is crucial for T-cell development and function, mutations in CD3 genes (like *CD3δ*, *CD3ε*, *CD3γ*, *CD3ζ*) cause SCID.
- **Link to B cells:** While the primary defect is in the TCR signaling pathway, severe defects in T-cell function (like those caused by CD3 mutations) often lead to secondary B-cell defects. T-cell help (via CD40L-CD40 interaction and cytokine secretion) is crucial for B-cell activation, proliferation, class switching, and memory formation. Without functional T cells, B cells cannot mount an effective antibody response, leading to panhypogammaglobulinemia and lack of B cells in the periphery. The patient's B cells also fail to respond to stimulation, which could be due to the lack of T-cell help or potentially a direct, albeit less severe, effect on BCR signaling components that interact with the CD3 pathway.
- **Kaplan/First Aid Correlation:** Kaplan Preclinical Immunology and Microbiology Review 2023 (pp. 118–128) and First Aid 2025 (Immunology: Primary Immunodeficiency Diseases) describe SCID caused by defects in lymphocyte receptor signaling, including mutations in CD3 genes. They emphasize the importance of the CD3 complex for TCR signaling and its role in T-cell development and function.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to a mutation in the *ZAP70* gene.** ZAP70 is a crucial kinase downstream of the TCR ζ chain ITAMs. Mutations in *ZAP70* also cause SCID with impaired T-cell and NK-cell function (T-B+NK+ SCID). While this involves defective TCR signaling, the vignette specifically points to a *CD3δ* mutation. ZAP70 deficiency is a distinct genetic cause, although clinically similar. The genetic finding in the vignette rules this out.
- (B) **Impaired B-cell development due to a mutation in the *BTK* gene.** Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) is essential for B-cell development beyond the pre-B cell stage. Mutations in *BTK* cause X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), characterized by a lack of mature B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia, but T-cell numbers and function are normal. This patient has both T-cell and B-cell defects, making XLA inconsistent with the findings.
- (C) **Defective immunoglobulin class switching due to a mutation in the *AID* gene.** Activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID) is required for immunoglobulin class switch recombination (CSR) and somatic hypermutation (SHM). Mutations in *AID* cause Hyper-IgM syndrome type 2, characterized by normal or elevated IgM levels but very low levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE. This patient has low levels of *all* immunoglobulin isotypes, including IgM, making AID deficiency unlikely.
- (E) **Defective B-cell maturation due to a mutation in the *μ* heavy chain gene.** Mutations in the *μ* heavy chain gene (IGHM) can cause defects in B-cell development, often leading to a block at the pre-B cell stage. This would result in a lack of mature B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia. However, T-cell function would be normal. This patient has both T-cell and B-cell defects, and the specific genetic defect identified is in *CD3δ*, not *IGHM*.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | CD3δ Mutation (SCID) | ZAP70 Mutation (SCID) | BTK Mutation (XLA) | AID Mutation (Hyper-IgM II) | μ Heavy Chain Mutation |
| :---------------------- | :------------------- | :-------------------- | :----------------- | :-------------------------- | :---------------------- |
| **T Cells**             | Absent/Non-functional | Absent/Non-functional  | Normal             | Normal                      | Normal                  |
| **B Cells**             | Absent/Non-functional | Normal                | Absent             | Normal                      | Absent                  |
| **NK Cells**            | Normal               | Absent/Non-functional  | Normal             | Normal                      | Normal                  |
| **Immunoglobulins**     | Panhypogammaglobulinemia | Normal                | Panhypogammaglobulinemia | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE   | Panhypogammaglobulinemia |
| **Genetic Defect**      | *CD3δ*               | *ZAP70*               | *BTK*              | *AID*                       | *IGHM*                  |
| **TCR Signaling**       | Defective            | Defective             | Normal             | Normal                      | Normal                  |
| **BCR Signaling**       | Defective (secondary) | Normal                | Defective          | Normal                      | Defective               |
| **Inheritance**         | Autosomal Recessive  | Autosomal Recessive   | X-linked Recessive | Autosomal Recessive         | Autosomal Recessive     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) is characterized by profound defects in both T-cell and B-cell function, leading to severe susceptibility to infections early in life. Defects in lymphocyte receptor signaling pathways (TCR and BCR) are a major cause of SCID. Mutations in genes encoding components of the CD3 complex (*CD3δ*, *CD3ε*, *CD3γ*, *CD3ζ*) or downstream signaling molecules like ZAP70 impair TCR signaling, leading to T-cell development arrest and functional defects. This often secondarily affects B-cell function due to lack of T-cell help. Differentiating SCID from other primary immunodeficiencies like XLA (B-cell defect only) or Hyper-IgM syndromes (class switch defect) requires evaluating both T-cell and B-cell numbers and function, as well as immunoglobulin levels.

<a id='question-030'></a>

### Question 030: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 30 (Symptoms)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the clinical manifestations associated with specific primary immunodeficiencies, particularly those affecting B-cell development and function, leading to recurrent bacterial infections, especially with encapsulated organisms. (本题考察特定原发性免疫缺陷的临床表现，特别是影响B细胞发育和功能的缺陷，导致复发性细菌感染，尤其是包膜细菌。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a high fever, cough, and shortness of breath. His mother reports that he has had recurrent ear infections (otitis media) and pneumonia since infancy, often requiring hospitalization. He has not received any live vaccines due to concerns about his immune system. Physical examination reveals a pale, cachectic child with crackles auscultated in both lower lung fields. A chest X-ray confirms bilateral pneumonia. Blood cultures grow *Streptococcus pneumoniae*. Immunological workup reveals markedly reduced levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE, with normal levels of IgM. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant reduction in the number of B cells (CD19+ cells).

Which of the following is the most likely underlying defect in this patient?

(A) Defective T-cell receptor signaling leading to impaired T-cell help for B cells.
(B) Defective recombination of immunoglobulin heavy and light chain genes.
(C) Defective expression of the CD40 ligand on T cells.
(D) Defective expression of the common gamma chain (γc) shared by multiple cytokine receptors.
(E) Defective phagocyte chemotaxis due to mutations in the *MYD88* gene.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy. Age is important as many primary immunodeficiencies manifest in early childhood.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Recurrent bacterial infections (otitis media, pneumonia) since infancy, severe pneumonia requiring hospitalization. This points towards a significant defect in humoral immunity, as encapsulated bacteria like *S. pneumoniae* are typically cleared by antibody-mediated responses.
- **Vaccination History:** Avoidance of live vaccines suggests a known or suspected immunodeficiency.
- **Physical Exam:** Pale, cachectic, lung crackles consistent with severe pneumonia.
- **Microbiology:** *Streptococcus pneumoniae* isolated from blood culture. This is a classic encapsulated bacterium that causes severe infections in patients with impaired antibody production.
- **Immunology Labs:**
    - Markedly reduced IgG, IgA, IgE: Indicates a severe defect in B-cell differentiation into plasma cells or antibody production.
    - Normal IgM: This is a crucial clue. IgM is typically the first antibody produced during a primary immune response and is less dependent on T-cell help for class switching. Its presence suggests that B cells can still mature to a certain extent and produce IgM, but fail to undergo class switching or affinity maturation.
    - Flow cytometry: Significant reduction in B cells (CD19+). This indicates a defect in B-cell development or survival.
- **Synthesis:** The combination of recurrent encapsulated bacterial infections, panhypogammaglobulinemia (low IgG, IgA, IgE) with normal IgM, and reduced B-cell numbers strongly points towards a defect in B-cell development or maturation, specifically affecting the transition from pre-B/immature B cells to mature B cells capable of class switching and producing diverse antibodies.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **B-cell Development:** B-cell development occurs primarily in the bone marrow. Pro-B cells rearrange the heavy chain locus (V(D)J recombination). Successful rearrangement leads to expression of the μ heavy chain, forming a pre-B cell receptor (pre-BCR). Pre-B cells then rearrange the light chain locus (V(J) recombination). Successful rearrangement leads to a complete IgM molecule on the surface of immature B cells. Immature B cells undergo negative selection (apoptosis if they bind self-antigens strongly) and then mature into transitional B cells, which migrate to the periphery. In the periphery, they encounter antigens and T-cell help (via CD40L-CD40 interaction and cytokines) to undergo class switch recombination (CSR) and somatic hypermutation (SHM), leading to the production of IgG, IgA, IgE, and high-affinity antibodies.
- **X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) / Bruton's Agammaglobulinemia:** This is caused by mutations in the *BTK* gene, which encodes Bruton's tyrosine kinase. BTK is essential for B-cell development beyond the pre-B cell stage. Patients with XLA have very low or absent B cells (CD19+) and consequently, very low levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE). This does not fit the patient's profile of normal IgM.
- **Hyper-IgM Syndromes:** These are characterized by normal or elevated IgM levels but low levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE. This is due to defects in class switch recombination (CSR).
    - **CD40L deficiency (X-linked Hyper-IgM Syndrome):** Mutations in the *CD40LG* gene prevent T cells from expressing CD40L. CD40L on activated T cells binds to CD40 on B cells, providing a crucial signal for CSR and affinity maturation. Patients have low IgG, IgA, IgE, normal IgM, and reduced B cells. They are susceptible to opportunistic infections (like *Pneumocystis jirovecii*) due to impaired T-cell interactions.
    - **CD40 deficiency (Autosomal Recessive Hyper-IgM Syndrome):** Mutations in the *CD40* gene prevent B cells from expressing CD40. This also impairs CSR, leading to low IgG, IgA, IgE, normal IgM.
    - **AID deficiency:** Activation-Induced Cytidine Deaminase (AID) is essential for both CSR and SHM. Deficiency leads to low IgG, IgA, IgE, normal IgM, and impaired affinity maturation.
    - **Uracil-DNA Glycosylase (UNG) deficiency:** Involved in CSR. Deficiency leads to low IgG, IgA, IgE, normal IgM.
- **Defective Recombination (e.g., RAG1/2 deficiency, Artemis deficiency):** Mutations in genes involved in V(D)J recombination (like *RAG1*, *RAG2*, *Artemis*, *DCLRE1C*) impair the development of both T and B cells, leading to Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID). Patients have very low T and B cell numbers and panhypogammaglobulinemia (low levels of all isotypes). This doesn't fit the normal IgM and reduced B cells profile.
- **Common Variable Immunodeficiency (CVID):** A heterogeneous group of disorders characterized by low IgG and often low IgA and/or IgM, impaired antibody responses, and recurrent infections. B-cell numbers are usually normal or slightly reduced. Onset is typically later than infancy. This doesn't fit the early onset and specific lab findings.

- **Connecting to the Case:** The patient has recurrent bacterial infections, low IgG, IgA, IgE, normal IgM, and reduced B cells. This pattern is most consistent with a defect in B-cell development *after* the IgM stage but *before* full maturation and class switching. Defects in V(D)J recombination (like RAG deficiency) would affect both T and B cells and typically result in low IgM as well. XLA would result in very low B cells and low levels of *all* immunoglobulins. CVID usually presents later and has normal B cell numbers. The combination of low IgG/IgA/IgE, normal IgM, and reduced B cells points strongly towards a defect in the later stages of B-cell maturation or class switching, potentially involving recombination processes or signaling pathways required for maturation beyond the IgM stage. However, the most classic presentation matching *low IgG/IgA/IgE, normal IgM, and reduced B cells* is often associated with defects affecting the transition from immature to mature B cells or early class switching events, which can sometimes be linked to recombination issues or signaling defects impacting B cell survival and maturation. Among the choices, defective recombination of immunoglobulin genes (B) is the most plausible explanation for a block in B-cell maturation leading to reduced B cells and inability to produce IgG, IgA, IgE while still allowing some IgM production. While Hyper-IgM syndromes (C) fit the immunoglobulin profile, they typically have normal or increased B cell numbers, not reduced numbers.

##### 3. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **V(D)J Recombination:** This process is crucial for generating diversity in T-cell receptors (TCRs) and B-cell receptors (BCRs, i.e., immunoglobulins). It involves the somatic recombination of gene segments (Variable, Diversity, Joining) encoded by the RAG1 and RAG2 enzymes. Defects in RAG1/2 or other recombination-associated genes (e.g., Artemis, DCLRE1C) lead to SCID, characterized by a lack of mature T and B cells and severe immunodeficiency. While SCID typically presents with low IgM, some atypical presentations or partial defects might theoretically lead to reduced B cells and impaired class switching with relatively preserved IgM, although this is less common than the classic SCID phenotype.
- **CD40L-CD40 Interaction:** Essential for T-cell dependent B-cell activation, CSR, and affinity maturation. Deficiency leads to Hyper-IgM syndrome.
- **Common Gamma Chain (γc):** Shared by receptors for IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21. Deficiency causes X-linked SCID (X-SCID), characterized by absent T cells and NK cells, and very low B cells, with severe infections.
- **MYD88:** An adaptor protein essential for signaling downstream of TLRs and IL-1 receptors. Deficiency causes susceptibility to pyogenic bacterial infections, but not typically the specific immunoglobulin profile seen here.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Defective T-cell receptor signaling leading to impaired T-cell help for B cells. This would primarily affect T-cell function and T-cell dependent B-cell activation, CSR, and affinity maturation. While it could lead to low IgG/IgA/IgE, IgM levels might be normal or even elevated, and B-cell numbers are usually normal. This doesn't fit the reduced B-cell count.
- (C) Defective expression of the CD40 ligand on T cells. This causes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have low IgG, IgA, IgE, but *normal or elevated* IgM levels and typically *normal or increased* B-cell numbers. This contradicts the patient's normal IgM and reduced B-cell count.
- (D) Defective expression of the common gamma chain (γc) shared by multiple cytokine receptors. This causes X-linked SCID. Patients have absent T cells and NK cells, and very low B cells. They have panhypogammaglobulinemia (low levels of all isotypes, including IgM). This contradicts the patient's normal IgM level.
- (E) Defective phagocyte chemotaxis due to mutations in the *MYD88* gene. *MYD88* deficiency causes susceptibility to pyogenic bacterial infections (like *S. pneumoniae*, *S. aureus*) due to impaired TLR signaling in phagocytes. It does not directly affect B-cell development, immunoglobulin production, or B-cell numbers. This doesn't explain the specific immunoglobulin profile or reduced B cells.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | XLA (BTK defect) | Hyper-IgM (CD40L/CD40/AID defect) | SCID (RAG/γc defect) | Patient Case |
|----------------------|-------------------|-----------------------------------|----------------------|---------------|
| IgG, IgA, IgE       | Very Low          | Very Low                          | Very Low             | Low           |
| IgM                 | Very Low          | Normal/High                        | Very Low             | Normal        |
| B-cell Count (CD19+) | Very Low/Absent   | Normal/High                        | Very Low/Absent      | Low           |
| T-cell Count        | Normal            | Normal                            | Very Low/Absent      | Normal        |
| Primary Defect      | B-cell maturation | Class Switch Recombination        | V(D)J Recombination/Cytokine Signaling | B-cell maturation/Recombination |
| Typical Infections  | Encapsulated bacteria | Opportunistic (PCP), Encapsulated bacteria | Severe, broad range | Encapsulated bacteria |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of recurrent bacterial infections, particularly with encapsulated organisms, combined with specific immunoglobulin patterns (low IgG/IgA/IgE, normal IgM) and B-cell counts, is crucial for diagnosing primary immunodeficiencies. Defects in V(D)J recombination can lead to reduced B-cell numbers and impaired immunoglobulin production, although classic SCID usually involves low IgM. Hyper-IgM syndromes present with low IgG/IgA/IgE but normal/high IgM and normal B cells. XLA presents with very low B cells and low levels of all immunoglobulins. Understanding these key distinctions is essential for USMLE Step 1.
