<a id='question-039'></a>

### Question 039: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 39 (disorders)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on the molecular defect, clinical presentation, and associated infections characteristic of X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), also known as Bruton's agammaglobulinemia, caused by mutations in the *BTK* gene, leading to a failure of B-cell development and maturation. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷病，特别是布鲁顿无γ-球蛋白血症 (XLA)，其分子缺陷、临床表现和相关感染，该病由 *BTK* 基因突变引起，导致 B 细胞发育和成熟失败。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to his pediatrician with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including otitis media, sinusitis, and pneumonia, starting around 6 months of age after maternal antibody protection waned. He has not received any live vaccines. Physical examination reveals normal growth and development, but no palpable lymph nodes or tonsils. Laboratory studies show markedly decreased serum levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE) and a severely reduced number of peripheral B cells (<1% of lymphocytes). T-cell numbers and function are normal. A specific genetic test reveals a mutation in the *BTK* gene located on the X chromosome.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological defect in this patient?

(A) Defective T-cell receptor signaling leading to impaired T-cell activation and cytokine production.
(B) Impaired formation of the pre-B-cell receptor (pre-BCR) complex, preventing B-cell maturation beyond the pre-B-cell stage.
(C) Defective class switching recombination due to a deficiency in activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID).
(D) Impaired phagocyte chemotaxis and oxidative burst due to a defect in NADPH oxidase.
(E) Defective complement component C3 synthesis leading to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy. This age is typical for the onset of symptoms in XLA after maternal antibodies wane (around 6 months). The male sex points towards an X-linked disorder.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Recurrent sinopulmonary infections (otitis media, sinusitis, pneumonia) starting around 6 months. This pattern is characteristic of antibody deficiency, as maternal IgG provides protection initially, but the patient cannot produce their own antibodies.
- **Physical Exam:** Absence of palpable lymph nodes and tonsils. B-cell follicles are responsible for the structure of these lymphoid tissues; their absence indicates a lack of B cells.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Markedly decreased serum levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE). This panhypogammaglobulinemia confirms a severe B-cell defect. Severely reduced number of peripheral B cells (<1%). This confirms the absence of mature B cells. Normal T-cell numbers and function. This rules out combined immunodeficiencies (like SCID) or T-cell specific defects.
- **Genetic Test:** Mutation in the *BTK* gene on the X chromosome. This is the definitive genetic cause of XLA.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying immunological defect.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA):** Caused by mutations in the *BTK* (Bruton's tyrosine kinase) gene. BTK is crucial for B-cell development.
- **B-cell Development Pathway:** Hematopoietic stem cell -> Pro-B cell -> Pre-B cell -> Immature B cell -> Mature B cell.
- **Role of BTK:** BTK is essential for signaling downstream of the pre-B-cell receptor (pre-BCR) and B-cell receptor (BCR). The pre-BCR is formed by the heavy chain (μ) and surrogate light chain (VpreB + λ5). Successful signaling through the pre-BCR (mediated by BTK) is required for the B cell to proliferate and undergo allelic exclusion (rearranging the light chain locus). Without functional BTK, signaling through the pre-BCR is defective.
- **Consequence of BTK Deficiency:** B-cell development arrests at the pre-B-cell stage in the bone marrow. Consequently, mature B cells are absent in the periphery, and immunoglobulin production is severely impaired (panhypogammaglobulinemia). T-cell development and function are unaffected.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** Recurrent bacterial infections, particularly with encapsulated organisms (e.g., *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*), starting after 6 months of age. Patients are susceptible to enteroviruses (like poliovirus) and Giardia lamblia.
- **Diagnosis:** Low/absent serum immunoglobulins, absent B cells in peripheral blood (CD19+ cells <1%), normal T-cell numbers and function, and *BTK* gene mutation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective T-cell receptor signaling leading to impaired T-cell activation and cytokine production.** This describes defects in T-cell signaling pathways (e.g., ZAP-70 deficiency) or T-cell development (e.g., DiGeorge syndrome). The vignette clearly states normal T-cell numbers and function, ruling this out.
- (C) **Defective class switching recombination due to a deficiency in activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID).** AID deficiency causes Hyper-IgM syndrome type 2. Patients have normal or elevated IgM levels but deficient IgG, IgA, and IgE. B-cell numbers are typically normal. This contradicts the panhypogammaglobulinemia and absent B cells seen in the patient.
- (D) **Impaired phagocyte chemotaxis and oxidative burst due to a defect in NADPH oxidase.** This describes Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). CGD affects phagocytes, leading to recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms (e.g., *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Aspergillus*). B-cell numbers and immunoglobulin levels are normal in CGD.
- (E) **Defective complement component C3 synthesis leading to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria.** C3 deficiency is a component deficiency of the complement system. While it increases susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria, it does not cause panhypogammaglobulinemia or absent B cells. B-cell development and immunoglobulin production are typically normal.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | XLA (BTK Deficiency) | Hyper-IgM Syndrome (AID Deficiency) | CGD (NADPH Oxidase Defect) | C3 Deficiency |
| :---------------------- | :------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :------------------------- | :------------ |
| **Primary Defect**      | B-cell development   | Class Switch Recombination        | Phagocyte Function         | Complement    |
| **Gene Involved**       | *BTK* (X-linked)     | *AID* (Autosomal Recessive)       | *CYBB* (X-linked) or others | *C3* (Autosomal) |
| **B-cell Count**        | Absent (<1%)         | Normal                             | Normal                    | Normal        |
| **Ig Levels**           | Panhypogammaglobulinemia (↓IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE) | ↑IgM, ↓IgG, IgA, IgE        | Normal                    | Normal        |
| **T-cell Count/Function** | Normal               | Normal                             | Normal                    | Normal        |
| **Typical Infections**  | Encapsulated bacteria | Opportunistic (PCP, Crypto)        | Catalase+ bacteria, fungi | Encapsulated bacteria |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
XLA is characterized by a block in B-cell development at the pre-B-cell stage due to defective BTK signaling, leading to absent B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia. Key differentials include other primary antibody deficiencies (like CVID, selective IgA deficiency, Hyper-IgM syndromes) and T-cell/combined immunodeficiencies. Remember the specific defect (pre-BCR signaling) and its consequence (arrested B-cell maturation).

### Question 040: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 40 (pediatric)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on the molecular defect and resulting clinical manifestations of Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS), a combined immunodeficiency characterized by eczema, thrombocytopenia, and recurrent infections, due to mutations in the WAS gene affecting actin cytoskeleton regulation in hematopoietic cells. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷病，特别是Wiskott-Aldrich综合征（WAS），一种联合免疫缺陷，其特征是湿疹、血小板减少症和复发性感染，这是由于WAS基因突变导致造血细胞中肌动蛋白细胞骨架调节受损所致。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant presents to the pediatrician with a history of recurrent otitis media, pneumonia, and severe eczema since birth. His mother reports frequent bleeding episodes, including easy bruising and prolonged bleeding from minor cuts. Physical examination reveals petechiae on the lower extremities, dry and scaly skin consistent with eczema, and mild splenomegaly. Laboratory studies show thrombocytopenia (platelet count 25,000/µL) with small platelets, low serum IgM levels, and normal serum IgG and IgA levels. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals decreased expression of CD43 (BCR signaling molecule) and absent expression of CD62L (L-selectin). Which of the following molecular defects is most likely responsible for this patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells, impairing isotype switching in B cells.
(B) Defective WASp protein, leading to impaired actin polymerization and cytoskeletal rearrangement in hematopoietic cells.
(C) Defective Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) signaling, affecting cytokine receptor signaling in lymphocytes.
(D) Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) expression, impairing B cell development and maturation.
(E) Defective adenosine deaminase (ADA) activity, leading to accumulation of toxic metabolites in lymphocytes.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 6-month-old male infant. This age is typical for the onset of symptoms in many primary immunodeficiencies. Male sex is relevant as WAS is X-linked recessive.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Recurrent infections (otitis media, pneumonia), severe eczema, and bleeding diathesis (easy bruising, prolonged bleeding, petechiae). This triad is highly suggestive of Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Thrombocytopenia with *small* platelets is a hallmark of WAS. Low IgM with normal IgG/IgA suggests a defect in humoral immunity, particularly affecting IgM production.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Decreased CD43 and absent CD62L expression on lymphocytes are characteristic findings in WAS. CD43 is involved in T cell activation and signaling, while CD62L is crucial for lymphocyte homing and adhesion.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying molecular defect responsible for this constellation of findings.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS):** An X-linked recessive primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene located on the X chromosome (Xp11.23).
- **WASp Protein:** The *WAS* gene encodes the Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome protein (WASp), a cytoplasmic protein crucial for actin cytoskeleton organization and signaling in hematopoietic cells, particularly lymphocytes and platelets.
- **Mechanism of Action:** WASp interacts with various signaling molecules and actin-binding proteins (like WAVE/Scar complex, Arp2/3 complex) to regulate actin polymerization, cell motility, adhesion, and synapse formation.
- **Consequences of WASp Deficiency:**
    - **Hematopoietic Cells:** Impaired actin dynamics lead to defective platelet formation (small platelets, thrombocytopenia) and abnormal lymphocyte function (impaired T cell receptor signaling, defective chemotaxis, impaired synapse formation).
    - **Immune Dysfunction:** Combined immunodeficiency affecting both T and B cells. T cell dysfunction leads to impaired cell-mediated immunity and defective T cell help for B cells. B cell dysfunction results in low IgM levels, impaired antibody responses to polysaccharide antigens, and increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria.
    - **Clinical Manifestations:** The classic triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections (due to combined immunodeficiency). Increased risk of autoimmunity and malignancy (lymphoma).
- **Flow Cytometry Markers:** CD43 (sialophorin) expression is often reduced or absent on lymphocytes in WAS, as WASp is involved in its glycosylation and surface expression. CD62L (L-selectin) expression is also typically absent, affecting lymphocyte homing.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells:** This causes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have recurrent infections (especially opportunistic infections like *Pneumocystis jirovecii*), but typically *lack* eczema and thrombocytopenia. They have normal or elevated IgM levels but low IgG, IgA, and IgE due to impaired isotype switching. This doesn't fit the patient's low IgM, eczema, and thrombocytopenia.
- (C) **Defective Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) signaling:** This causes X-linked Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (X-SCID). Patients present early in infancy with severe, life-threatening infections (bacterial, viral, fungal, opportunistic). They have profound lymphopenia (especially T cells and NK cells) and absent T cell function. Eczema and thrombocytopenia are not typical features.
- (D) **Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) expression:** This causes X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA). Patients present after 6 months (when maternal IgG wanes) with recurrent bacterial infections, particularly with encapsulated organisms (*Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*). They have very low levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM) and absent B cells. Eczema and thrombocytopenia are not features.
- (E) **Defective adenosine deaminase (ADA) activity:** This causes ADA-SCID, an autosomal recessive form of Severe Combined Immunodeficiency. Patients present early with severe infections. ADA deficiency leads to the accumulation of toxic metabolites (deoxyadenosine) that are particularly toxic to lymphocytes, causing severe lymphopenia (T, B, and NK cells). Eczema and thrombocytopenia are not typical features.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS) | X-linked Hyper-IgM (CD40L def.) | X-linked SCID (JAK3 def.) | XLA (BTK def.) | ADA-SCID |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|----------------------------|-----------------|----------|
| Inheritance          | X-linked Recessive              | X-linked Recessive                | X-linked Recessive         | X-linked Recessive | Autosomal Recessive |
| Age of Onset         | Infancy                         | Infancy                           | Early Infancy              | >6 months       | Early Infancy |
| Infections           | Recurrent (bacterial, viral)    | Recurrent (esp. opportunistic)    | Severe, life-threatening   | Recurrent bacterial | Severe, life-threatening |
| Eczema               | Yes                             | No                                | No                        | No              | No       |
| Thrombocytopenia     | Yes (small platelets)           | No                                | No                        | No              | No       |
| IgM Levels           | Low                             | Normal/High                       | Normal                     | Very Low        | Normal   |
| IgG/IgA Levels       | Normal                          | Low                               | Low                        | Very Low        | Low      |
| Lymphopenia          | Mild/Moderate                   | No                                | Severe (T, NK)             | No              | Severe (T, B, NK) |
| Key Defect           | WASp (actin cytoskeleton)       | CD40L (T cell help for B cells)   | JAK3 (cytokine signaling)  | BTK (B cell dev.) | ADA (purine metabolism) |
| Flow Cytometry       | ↓CD43, absent CD62L             | Normal                            | Absent T/NK cells          | Absent B cells  | Normal   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome is characterized by the triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections due to a defect in the WASp protein, which disrupts actin cytoskeleton regulation. Differentiating WAS from other primary immunodeficiencies like X-linked Hyper-IgM (normal IgM, no thrombocytopenia/eczema), X-linked SCID (severe lymphopenia, no thrombocytopenia/eczema), and XLA (absent B cells, no thrombocytopenia/eczema) relies on recognizing this specific clinical triad and associated laboratory findings (small platelets, low IgM, flow cytometry markers).

<a id='question-041'></a>

### Question 041: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 41 (nature)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in lymphocyte development and function, particularly related to T-cell maturation and the role of specific signaling pathways (e.g., JAK-STAT pathway) in immune cell development and function. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是淋巴细胞发育和功能缺陷，重点关注T细胞成熟以及特定信号通路（如JAK-STAT通路）在免疫细胞发育和功能中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant presents with recurrent severe bacterial infections, including pneumonia, otitis media, and sepsis, starting shortly after maternal antibodies waned. He has failed to thrive and exhibits chronic diarrhea. Physical examination reveals lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly. Laboratory investigations show markedly reduced numbers of T lymphocytes (CD3+ cells) and normal numbers of B lymphocytes (CD19+ cells) and NK cells (CD56+ cells). Flow cytometry reveals that the few T cells present are immature and lack expression of CD4 and CD8. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *JAK3* gene.

Which of the following immunological defects is most likely responsible for this patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Defective B-cell maturation leading to agammaglobulinemia.
(B) Impaired T-cell receptor signaling due to mutations in the CD3 complex.
(C) Defective cytokine receptor signaling preventing lymphocyte development.
(D) Deficient complement component C3 leading to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria.
(E) Impaired phagocyte function due to a defect in NADPH oxidase.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 6-month-old male infant. Age is significant as maternal antibodies typically wane around this time, unmasking underlying immunodeficiencies.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Recurrent severe bacterial infections (pneumonia, otitis media, sepsis), failure to thrive, chronic diarrhea. These symptoms point towards a severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or a severe T-cell defect.
- **Physical Exam:** Lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly can be seen in various immunodeficiencies, including SCID, often due to chronic infections or lymphoid hyperplasia.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Markedly reduced T lymphocytes (CD3+), normal B lymphocytes (CD19+), normal NK cells (CD56+). This pattern suggests a defect primarily affecting T-cell development or survival, sparing B and NK cells. The presence of immature T cells lacking CD4/CD8 further supports a defect early in T-cell maturation.
- **Genetic Finding:** Homozygous mutation in the *JAK3* gene. This is the crucial piece of information. JAK3 is a tyrosine kinase essential for signaling downstream of several cytokine receptors, including those for the common gamma chain (γc), which is shared by receptors for IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the immunological defect responsible for the presentation.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **JAK-STAT Pathway:** The JAK-STAT pathway is crucial for signal transduction from cytokine receptors to the nucleus. Cytokines bind to their receptors, leading to receptor dimerization and activation of associated Janus kinases (JAKs). JAKs phosphorylate the receptor and STAT proteins (Signal Transducers and Activators of Transcription). Phosphorylated STATs dimerize, translocate to the nucleus, and regulate gene expression.
- **JAK3 Function:** JAK3 is specifically expressed in hematopoietic cells and is essential for signaling through cytokine receptors that utilize the common gamma chain (γc). These cytokines (IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, IL-21) are critical for lymphocyte development and function.
- **IL-7 and T-cell Development:** IL-7, signaling through the IL-7 receptor (which contains the γc), is essential for the survival and proliferation of T-cell precursors in the thymus. Without functional JAK3, IL-7 signaling is disrupted, leading to a block in T-cell development at an early stage (double-negative stage), resulting in severe T-cell lymphopenia.
- **IL-15 and NK-cell Development:** IL-15, also signaling through a receptor containing the γc, is crucial for the development and survival of NK cells. While NK cells are typically normal in JAK3 deficiency, severe defects can sometimes occur.
- **IL-2, IL-4, IL-9, IL-21 and Lymphocyte Function:** IL-2 is critical for T-cell proliferation and differentiation. IL-4 is important for B-cell class switching to IgE and Th2 differentiation. IL-9 and IL-21 have roles in various immune responses. Defects in these pathways contribute to the overall immunodeficiency.
- **X-linked SCID (X-SCID):** Mutations in the *IL2RG* gene, which encodes the common gamma chain (γc), cause X-linked SCID. This is the most common form of SCID. Patients with X-SCID have defects in signaling through IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21 receptors. Clinically, they present with severe T-cell lymphopenia, variable B-cell and NK-cell numbers (often reduced), and recurrent severe infections.
- **Autosomal Recessive SCID due to JAK3 Deficiency:** Mutations in the *JAK3* gene cause an autosomal recessive form of SCID with a similar phenotype to X-SCID because JAK3 is required for signaling through all γc-dependent cytokine receptors. The clinical presentation described in the vignette (severe T-cell lymphopenia, normal B and NK cells, early-onset severe infections) is characteristic of SCID, and the *JAK3* mutation specifically points to a defect in cytokine receptor signaling essential for lymphocyte development.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Defective B-cell maturation leading to agammaglobulinemia. This describes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), caused by mutations in the *BTK* gene. Patients with XLA have normal T-cell numbers but lack mature B cells and immunoglobulins, leading to recurrent bacterial infections, particularly with encapsulated organisms. The vignette clearly shows normal B-cell numbers and severe T-cell deficiency, ruling out XLA.
- (B) Impaired T-cell receptor signaling due to mutations in the CD3 complex. Mutations in genes encoding components of the CD3 complex (e.g., *TCRZ*, *TCRB*, *CD3D*, *CD3E*) cause defects in T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling. This leads to impaired T-cell development and function, often resulting in T-cell lymphopenia. However, the specific genetic finding of a *JAK3* mutation points directly to a cytokine signaling defect, not a TCR signaling defect. While both affect T cells, the underlying mechanism is different.
- (D) Deficient complement component C3 leading to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria. C3 deficiency is a severe complement deficiency that impairs opsonization and bacterial lysis. Patients are highly susceptible to recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*). While the patient has bacterial infections, the primary defect is in lymphocyte development (specifically T cells), not the complement system. Complement deficiencies typically present with normal lymphocyte counts.
- (E) Impaired phagocyte function due to a defect in NADPH oxidase. This describes Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). Patients with CGD have defective phagocytes that cannot produce reactive oxygen species to kill ingested microbes. They are susceptible to infections with catalase-positive organisms (e.g., *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Aspergillus*). The vignette describes severe T-cell lymphopenia and normal phagocyte function is assumed unless otherwise stated. The primary defect is clearly in the adaptive immune system (lymphocytes).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | JAK3 Deficiency (X-SCID) | XLA (BTK mutation) | CD3 Deficiency | C3 Deficiency | CGD (NADPH Oxidase Defect) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------- | :------------- | :------------ | :------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Cytokine Signaling       | B-cell Maturation  | TCR Signaling  | Complement    | Phagocyte Function         |
| **Gene Affected**       | *JAK3*                   | *BTK*              | *CD3* complex  | *C3*          | *CYBB* (most common)       |
| **T-cell Count**        | Severely Reduced         | Normal             | Reduced        | Normal        | Normal                    |
| **B-cell Count**        | Normal                   | Absent             | Normal         | Normal        | Normal                    |
| **NK-cell Count**       | Normal (usually)          | Normal             | Normal         | Normal        | Normal                    |
| **Immunoglobulin Levels** | Low (due to lack of T help) | Very Low/Absent    | Low            | Normal        | Normal                    |
| **Key Infections**      | Broad (bacterial, viral, fungal) | Encapsulated bacteria | Broad          | Encapsulated bacteria | Catalase+ bacteria, fungi  |
| **Inheritance**         | Autosomal Recessive      | X-linked           | Autosomal Recessive/Dominant | Autosomal Recessive | X-linked (most common)    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The vignette describes a classic presentation of Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) characterized by severe T-cell lymphopenia, normal B and NK cells, and early-onset severe infections. The specific genetic finding of a *JAK3* mutation points to a defect in cytokine receptor signaling, which is essential for lymphocyte development. This condition mimics X-linked SCID (due to γc deficiency) clinically and immunologically. Key differentials include other forms of SCID (e.g., ADA deficiency, RAG deficiency), XLA (B-cell defect), CGD (phagocyte defect), and complement deficiencies. Understanding the specific genetic defect and its impact on signaling pathways is crucial for diagnosis.

<a id='question-042'></a>

### Question 042: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 42 (appear)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular basis and clinical manifestations of Primary Immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in lymphocyte development and function, particularly related to the common gamma chain (γc) shared by multiple cytokine receptors, leading to impaired T-cell, NK-cell, and B-cell development/function. (考察原发性免疫缺陷症的分子基础和临床表现，特别是共同γ链(γc)缺陷，导致T细胞、NK细胞和B细胞的发育/功能受损。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant, born full-term without complications, presents with recurrent severe infections starting at 3 months of age. He has had multiple episodes of pneumonia (diagnosed clinically and treated with antibiotics), persistent oral thrush unresponsive to antifungal therapy, and failure to thrive. Family history is significant for a maternal uncle who died in infancy from an unknown illness. Physical examination reveals a cachectic infant with hepatosplenomegaly and diffuse lymphadenopathy. Laboratory studies show severe lymphopenia (absolute lymphocyte count 200/µL), markedly reduced T-cell numbers (CD3+ T cells < 100/µL), absent NK cells (CD56+), and normal B-cell numbers (CD19+), although immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM) are very low. Flow cytometry reveals absent expression of the common gamma chain (γc) on lymphocytes.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying defect in this patient?

(A) Defective recombination activating genes (RAG1/RAG2) leading to impaired V(D)J recombination.
(B) Defective Bruton tyrosine kinase (BTK) leading to impaired B-cell development.
(C) Defective Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) leading to impaired signaling downstream of multiple cytokine receptors.
(D) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) leading to impaired T-cell help for B-cell isotype switching.
(E) Defective inducible costimulator (ICOS) leading to impaired T-cell help for B-cell affinity maturation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age and Presentation:** 6-month-old infant with recurrent severe infections (pneumonia, thrush) starting around 3 months, failure to thrive. This timing is classic for Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) or related severe primary immunodeficiencies, as maternal antibodies wane.
- **Infection Types:** Pneumonia (bacterial, viral, fungal), persistent oral thrush (Candida, fungal). Suggests defects in both cellular (T-cell) and humoral (B-cell) immunity.
- **Family History:** Maternal uncle died in infancy from unknown illness. Suggests a possible X-linked inheritance pattern.
- **Physical Exam:** Cachexia, hepatosplenomegaly, lymphadenopathy. Common findings in SCID due to chronic infections and immune dysregulation.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Severe lymphopenia: Overall low lymphocyte count.
    - Markedly reduced T-cells (CD3+): Indicates a T-cell deficiency.
    - Absent NK cells (CD56+): Indicates an NK-cell deficiency.
    - Normal B-cells (CD19+): Suggests B-cell precursors are present, but function is likely impaired due to lack of T-cell help or intrinsic defect.
    - Low Immunoglobulins (IgG, IgA, IgM): Confirms humoral immunodeficiency, likely secondary to T-cell defect or intrinsic B-cell issue.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Absent expression of the common gamma chain (γc) on lymphocytes. This is the definitive clue pointing to the specific molecular defect.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Common Gamma Chain (γc):** The γc (encoded by the *IL2RG* gene on the X chromosome) is a crucial signaling subunit shared by receptors for several key cytokines: IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21.
- **Cytokine Receptor Signaling:** These cytokines are essential for the development and function of lymphocytes.
    - IL-7 is critical for T-cell and NK-cell development in the bone marrow and thymus.
    - IL-15 is crucial for NK-cell survival and proliferation.
    - IL-2, IL-4, IL-9, IL-21 are important for T-cell proliferation, differentiation (e.g., Th2, Treg), and function, as well as B-cell function (e.g., class switching, antibody production).
- **JAK3:** The signaling pathway downstream of these receptors involves the Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) enzyme, which is constitutively associated with the intracellular domains of the γc-containing receptors. Upon cytokine binding, JAK3 is activated and phosphorylates signal transducer and activator of transcription (STAT) proteins, leading to gene transcription.
- **X-linked SCID (X-SCID):** Mutations in the *IL2RG* gene, leading to absent or non-functional γc, result in X-linked SCID. This is the most common form of SCID. The lack of γc prevents signaling through IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21 receptors.
- **Clinical Consequences of γc Deficiency:**
    - Impaired T-cell development (due to lack of IL-7 signaling).
    - Impaired NK-cell development and survival (due to lack of IL-7 and IL-15 signaling).
    - Impaired B-cell function (due to lack of T-cell help and direct effects of IL-4, IL-21). Although B-cell numbers may be normal initially, immunoglobulin production is severely deficient.
- **The patient's presentation:** Recurrent infections (bacterial, viral, fungal), failure to thrive, lymphopenia, absent T-cells and NK-cells, low immunoglobulins, and absent γc expression are all hallmarks of X-linked SCID due to *IL2RG* mutations. The X-linked inheritance is suggested by the affected maternal uncle.
- **JAK3 Deficiency:** JAK3 is the kinase directly downstream of the γc-containing receptors. Mutations in the *JAK3* gene cause autosomal recessive SCID with a similar phenotype to X-SCID (T- B+ NK- SCID), because JAK3 is essential for signaling through all these receptors. Therefore, a defect in JAK3 would also lead to the observed clinical and immunological findings.
- **Option C (Defective JAK3):** This is the correct answer. The absence of γc expression (as stated in the vignette) directly leads to the inability of JAK3 to be activated downstream, resulting in the observed immunodeficiency. While the vignette states absent γc *expression*, the functional consequence is the lack of JAK3 signaling. In the context of X-SCID (most common form fitting the vignette), the primary defect is γc, but the *functional* defect affecting the signaling pathway is JAK3. Given the options, JAK3 deficiency is the most accurate description of the underlying signaling defect causing the phenotype.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective RAG1/RAG2:** Recombination activating genes (RAG1 and RAG2) are essential for V(D)J recombination, the process that generates diversity in T-cell receptors (TCRs) and B-cell receptors (BCRs). Defects in RAG1/RAG2 cause SCID characterized by a lack of mature T-cells and B-cells (T- B- NK+ SCID). The patient has normal B-cell numbers (though non-functional) and absent T-cells and NK-cells. This phenotype does not match the vignette.
- (B) **Defective BTK:** Bruton tyrosine kinase (BTK) is crucial for B-cell development, specifically the transition from pro-B cells to pre-B cells in the bone marrow. Defects in BTK cause X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), characterized by a lack of mature B-cells and immunoglobulins, but normal T-cell numbers and function. The patient has normal B-cell numbers but absent T-cells and NK-cells, making this incorrect.
- (D) **Defective CD40L:** CD40 ligand (CD40L, encoded by the *CD40LG* gene on the X chromosome) is expressed on activated T-cells and interacts with CD40 on B-cells, antigen-presenting cells (APCs), and other cells. This interaction is essential for T-cell dependent B-cell activation, isotype switching, and germinal center formation. Defects in CD40L cause X-linked hyper-IgM syndrome, characterized by normal or elevated IgM levels but low IgG, IgA, and IgE levels, along with recurrent infections. T-cell numbers are typically normal. The patient has low levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes and absent T-cells, making this incorrect.
- (E) **Defective ICOS:** Inducible costimulator (ICOS) is a molecule expressed on activated T-cells that interacts with ICOS ligand (ICOSL) on B-cells and APCs. This interaction provides a crucial costimulatory signal for T-cell help to B-cells, particularly for affinity maturation and memory B-cell development. Defects in ICOS cause a form of Common Variable Immunodeficiency (CVID) or hyper-IgM syndrome, characterized by impaired antibody responses, particularly to polysaccharide antigens, and sometimes low B-cell numbers. T-cell numbers are typically normal. The patient has absent T-cells and NK-cells, making this incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | X-linked SCID (γc defect / JAK3 defect) | RAG Deficiency | XLA (BTK defect) | Hyper-IgM (CD40L defect) | ICOS Deficiency |
|----------------------|------------------------------------------|----------------|-----------------|--------------------------|----------------|
| Inheritance         | X-linked (most common) / Autosomal Recessive | Autosomal Recessive | X-linked       | X-linked                | Autosomal Dominant/Recessive |
| T-cells (CD3+)      | Absent/Very Low                          | Absent         | Normal          | Normal                  | Normal         |
| B-cells (CD19+)     | Normal/Low                               | Absent         | Absent          | Normal/Low               | Normal/Low     |
| NK-cells (CD56+)    | Absent/Very Low                          | Normal         | Normal          | Normal                  | Normal         |
| Immunoglobulins     | Very Low (all isotypes)                  | Very Low (all isotypes) | Very Low (all isotypes) | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE | Variable, often impaired affinity maturation |
| Key Defect          | IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-15, IL-21 signaling | V(D)J Recombination | B-cell development | T-cell help for B-cells | T-cell help for B-cells |
| Molecular Basis     | *IL2RG* mutation (γc) / *JAK3* mutation | *RAG1/RAG2* mutation | *BTK* mutation | *CD40LG* mutation       | *ICOS* mutation |
| Patient Phenotype   | Recurrent infections (viral, bacterial, fungal, opportunistic), FTT | Recurrent infections (viral, bacterial, opportunistic), FTT | Recurrent bacterial infections (encapsulated organisms) | Recurrent infections, opportunistic infections | Recurrent infections, impaired response to polysaccharide vaccines |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The most common form of SCID is X-linked SCID, caused by mutations in the *IL2RG* gene encoding the common gamma chain (γc). This leads to absent or non-functional receptors for IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21, resulting in impaired development of T-cells and NK-cells (due to lack of IL-7 signaling) and impaired B-cell function (due to lack of T-cell help and direct effects of cytokines like IL-4 and IL-21). The clinical presentation includes severe recurrent infections starting in early infancy, failure to thrive, and profound lymphopenia with absent T-cells and NK-cells. Autosomal recessive SCID can be caused by mutations in *JAK3*, which has a similar phenotype (T- B+ NK- SCID) because JAK3 is the kinase downstream of γc receptors. Differentiating SCID from other primary immunodeficiencies like XLA (B-cell defect), CVID (variable B-cell defect), or Hyper-IgM syndrome (T-cell help defect) requires careful analysis of lymphocyte subsets and immunoglobulin levels.

<a id='question-043'></a>

### Question 043: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 43 (usually)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Primary Immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in T-cell development and function, particularly DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome), and its associated immunological consequences, including susceptibility to specific opportunistic infections. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是T细胞发育和功能缺陷，例如DiGeorge综合征（22号染色体11.2区缺失），以及由此导致的对特定机会性感染的易感性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant presents to the emergency department with recurrent episodes of thrush (oral candidiasis) and failure to thrive. He was born full-term after an uncomplicated pregnancy but has a history of feeding difficulties since birth. Physical examination reveals a low-set jaw, hypertelorism, and a small thymus shadow on chest X-ray. Laboratory investigations show lymphopenia with a markedly reduced number of circulating T lymphocytes (CD3+ cells) and normal B lymphocyte counts (CD19+ cells). He is subsequently diagnosed with pneumonia caused by *Pneumocystis jirovecii*. Which of the following immunological defects is most likely responsible for this patient's clinical presentation and susceptibility to *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia?

(A) Defective complement component C3 leading to impaired opsonization and increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria.
(B) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells, impairing T-cell dependent B-cell activation and isotype switching.
(C) Defective development of the third and fourth pharyngeal pouches, leading to thymic hypoplasia and impaired T-cell maturation.
(D) Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) leading to impaired B-cell development and maturation.
(E) Defective interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) receptor signaling, impairing macrophage activation and host defense against intracellular pathogens.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 6-month-old male infant. Age is important as many primary immunodeficiencies manifest in infancy.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Recurrent thrush (oral candidiasis) and failure to thrive. These suggest a defect in cell-mediated immunity (CMI), as T cells are crucial for controlling fungal infections like *Candida* and maintaining adequate growth.
- **History:** Feeding difficulties since birth. This can be associated with congenital anomalies.
- **Physical Exam:** Low-set jaw, hypertelorism, small thymus shadow on chest X-ray. These are classic dysmorphic features associated with DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome). The small thymus shadow directly points to thymic hypoplasia/aplasia.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Lymphopenia, markedly reduced T lymphocytes (CD3+), normal B lymphocytes (CD19+). This confirms a T-cell deficiency while sparing B-cell development.
- **Infection:** *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia (PJP). PJP is a classic opportunistic infection seen in patients with severe T-cell deficiencies, particularly those affecting CD4+ T cells.
- **Synthesis:** The combination of dysmorphic features (low-set jaw, hypertelorism), thymic hypoplasia, T-cell lymphopenia, recurrent candidiasis, and PJP strongly points towards DiGeorge syndrome, which results from defective development of the third and fourth pharyngeal pouches.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome):** Caused by a microdeletion on chromosome 22q11.2. This region contains the *TBX1* gene, crucial for the development of the third and fourth pharyngeal pouches during embryogenesis.
- **Pharyngeal Pouch Development:** The third pharyngeal pouch gives rise to the thymus and inferior parathyroid glands. The fourth pharyngeal pouch gives rise to the ultimobranchial body (which contributes to the C cells of the thyroid) and the superior parathyroid glands.
- **Immunological Consequences:** Defective development of the third pharyngeal pouch leads to thymic hypoplasia or aplasia. The thymus is essential for T-cell maturation. Therefore, DiGeorge syndrome results in a severe deficiency of mature T lymphocytes (both CD4+ and CD8+). B-cell development is generally normal, although T-cell help is required for optimal B-cell function (isotype switching, affinity maturation, memory B-cell formation).
- **Clinical Manifestations:** Due to T-cell deficiency, patients are highly susceptible to opportunistic infections, especially viral, fungal (*Candida*, *Pneumocystis*), and intracellular bacterial infections. The lack of T-cell help can also lead to impaired antibody responses. Associated features include congenital heart defects (e.g., conotruncal abnormalities like tetralogy of Fallot, interrupted aortic arch), hypocalcemia (due to parathyroid hypoplasia), characteristic facial features (low-set ears, hypertelorism, micrognathia), and developmental delay.
- **Pneumocystis jirovecii:** An opportunistic fungus that causes pneumonia primarily in immunocompromised individuals, especially those with T-cell defects (e.g., HIV/AIDS, DiGeorge syndrome, post-transplant patients on immunosuppression).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective complement component C3:** C3 deficiency impairs the alternative and classical complement pathways, leading to reduced opsonization (C3b), chemotaxis (C3a), and membrane attack complex formation. Patients are primarily susceptible to recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*, *Neisseria meningitidis*). This does not explain the T-cell deficiency, thymic hypoplasia, dysmorphic features, or susceptibility to *Pneumocystis*.
- (B) **Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells (X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome):** CD40L on activated T cells binds to CD40 on B cells, macrophages, and dendritic cells. This interaction is crucial for T-cell dependent B-cell activation, isotype switching (IgM to IgG, IgA, IgE), and germinal center formation. Deficiency leads to normal or elevated IgM levels but very low levels of other isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgE). Patients are susceptible to opportunistic infections (like *Pneumocystis*) due to impaired T-cell help for macrophage activation and B-cell defects, but they typically have normal T-cell numbers and function, and lack the dysmorphic features and thymic hypoplasia seen in DiGeorge syndrome.
- (D) **Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) (X-linked agammaglobulinemia, XLA):** BTK is essential for B-cell development beyond the pre-B cell stage. Deficiency leads to a complete absence of mature B cells and immunoglobulins. Patients are highly susceptible to recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria, starting around 6 months of age when maternal antibodies wane. T-cell numbers and function are normal. This does not fit the patient's presentation of T-cell deficiency, thymic hypoplasia, or susceptibility to *Pneumocystis*.
- (E) **Defective interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) receptor signaling (IFN-γ receptor deficiency):** IFN-γ is a key cytokine produced by T cells and NK cells that activates macrophages to kill intracellular pathogens (e.g., *Mycobacterium*, *Salmonella*, *Listeria*). Deficiency leads to impaired macrophage activation and increased susceptibility to these specific intracellular bacteria and mycobacteria. T-cell numbers are usually normal, although T-cell function (specifically IFN-γ production) is impaired. This does not explain the T-cell lymphopenia, thymic hypoplasia, dysmorphic features, or susceptibility to *Candida* and *Pneumocystis* as the primary defect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 del) | C3 Deficiency        | CD40L Deficiency (HIGM) | BTK Deficiency (XLA) | IFN-γ Receptor Deficiency |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :------------------- | :---------------------- | :------------------- | :------------------------ |
| **Primary Defect**      | T-cell development (Thymus)    | Complement (C3)      | T-cell help (CD40L)     | B-cell development (BTK) | Macrophage Activation (IFN-γR) |
| **Cell Type Affected**  | T cells (CD3+)                 | N/A                  | T cells (CD40L), B cells | B cells (CD19+)     | Macrophages               |
| **Key Infections**      | *Candida*, *Pneumocystis*, Viruses | Encapsulated bacteria | *Pneumocystis*, Viruses | Encapsulated bacteria | *Mycobacteria*, *Salmonella*, *Listeria* |
| **Thymus**              | Hypoplastic/Aplastic           | Normal               | Normal                  | Normal               | Normal                    |
| **Immunoglobulins**     | Variable (T-cell help needed)  | Normal               | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE | Very Low All Ig     | Normal                    |
| **Dysmorphic Features** | Yes (Low-set jaw, hypertelorism) | No                   | No                      | No                   | No                        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
DiGeorge syndrome is a primary immunodeficiency characterized by defective development of the third and fourth pharyngeal pouches, leading to thymic hypoplasia/aplasia and T-cell deficiency. This results in susceptibility to opportunistic infections like *Candida* and *Pneumocystis*. Key clinical clues include dysmorphic features, thymic hypoplasia, T-cell lymphopenia, and associated congenital anomalies (e.g., heart defects, hypocalcemia). Differentiate from other primary immunodeficiencies based on the specific cell lineage affected (T cells, B cells, phagocytes, complement) and the resulting pattern of infections.

<a id='question-044'></a>

### Question 044: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 44 (around)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in T-cell development and function, leading to susceptibility to opportunistic infections, particularly those caused by intracellular pathogens and fungi. The key is recognizing the clinical presentation and immunological findings consistent with Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) or related T-cell defects. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是T细胞发育和功能缺陷，导致对机会性感染的易感性，尤其是由细胞内病原体和真菌引起的感染。关键在于识别与重症联合免疫缺陷（SCID）或相关T细胞缺陷相一致的临床表现和免疫学发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant, born at term to non-consanguineous parents, presents with recurrent severe infections starting at 2 months of age. He has had pneumonia (diagnosed clinically, treated with antibiotics), oral thrush unresponsive to standard antifungal therapy, and persistent diarrhea. Physical examination reveals failure to thrive (weight at the 3rd percentile), hepatosplenomegaly, and diffuse erythematous maculopapular rash. Laboratory studies show lymphopenia (absolute lymphocyte count 200/µL), markedly reduced T-cell numbers (CD3+ T cells < 100/µL), and normal B-cell numbers (CD19+ B cells 800/µL). Immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM) are low, consistent with maternal antibody decline but lack of active production. Flow cytometry reveals absent T-cell receptor (TCR) excision circles (TRECs) in peripheral blood lymphocytes.

(A) Deficiency in the complement component C3
(B) Defect in the CD40 ligand (CD40L) gene
(C) Mutation in the gene encoding the common gamma chain (γc)
(D) Deficiency in the enzyme adenosine deaminase (ADA)
(E) Defect in the Bruton tyrosine kinase (BTK) gene

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age and Presentation:** A 6-month-old infant presenting with recurrent, severe infections (pneumonia, thrush, diarrhea) starting early in life (2 months) is highly suggestive of a primary immunodeficiency, particularly one affecting cell-mediated immunity. The severity and broad range of pathogens (bacterial pneumonia, fungal thrush, potential viral/opportunistic diarrhea) point towards a profound defect.
- **Physical Exam:** Failure to thrive, hepatosplenomegaly, and rash are common findings in severe immunodeficiencies due to chronic infections and inflammation.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - **Lymphopenia:** Low absolute lymphocyte count indicates a problem with lymphocyte production or survival.
    - **Markedly Reduced T-cells (CD3+):** This is the most critical finding, pointing directly to a T-cell defect.
    - **Normal B-cells (CD19+):** This helps differentiate from B-cell deficiencies like X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA).
    - **Low Immunoglobulins:** While expected due to maternal antibody decline at this age, the lack of active B-cell response (given normal B-cell numbers) suggests impaired T-cell help, which is necessary for class switching and antibody production.
    - **Absent TRECs:** T-cell receptor excision circles (TRECs) are byproducts of T-cell receptor gene rearrangement during T-cell development in the thymus. Their absence indicates a severe defect in T-cell development, typically occurring within the thymus itself.
- **Synthesis:** The combination of early-onset severe infections (bacterial, fungal, potentially viral/opportunistic), profound T-cell lymphopenia, normal B-cell numbers, low immunoglobulins, and absent TRECs strongly points towards Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID). The question asks for the *most likely* underlying genetic defect among the options provided.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **SCID Overview:** SCID is a group of genetic disorders characterized by severe defects in both T-cell and B-cell function, leading to extreme susceptibility to infections. The most common forms involve defects in lymphocyte development or signaling.
- **Option (C) - Common Gamma Chain (γc) Deficiency:** The γc (encoded by the *IL2RG* gene on the X chromosome) is a shared signaling subunit for several cytokine receptors crucial for lymphocyte development and function, including IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21. IL-7 is essential for T-cell and NK-cell development, while IL-15 is important for NK-cell development and function. Defects in γc lead to a lack of T-cells and NK cells, with B-cells usually present but non-functional due to lack of T-cell help. This X-linked form is the most common cause of SCID (~50%). The clinical picture (early severe infections, T-cell lymphopenia, normal B-cells, low Ig) and the absent TRECs (indicating failed T-cell development) are perfectly consistent with γc deficiency.
- **Option (D) - Adenosine Deaminase (ADA) Deficiency:** ADA deficiency is an autosomal recessive form of SCID. ADA is an enzyme involved in purine metabolism. Its deficiency leads to the accumulation of toxic metabolites (deoxyadenosine and deoxy-ATP) primarily in lymphocytes, causing apoptosis and severe lymphopenia affecting T, B, and NK cells. While it causes SCID, it typically affects all lymphocyte lineages, not just T-cells, and the specific finding of absent TRECs is most characteristic of defects *within* the thymus affecting T-cell development itself, like γc deficiency.
- **Option (B) - CD40 Ligand (CD40L) Deficiency:** CD40L (encoded by the *CD40LG* gene on the X chromosome) is expressed on activated T-cells and interacts with CD40 on B-cells, antigen-presenting cells (APCs), and other cells. This interaction is crucial for T-cell dependent B-cell activation, class switching, and germinal center formation. Deficiency leads to X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal T-cell numbers and function but impaired B-cell function, resulting in low IgG, IgA, IgE, but normal or high IgM levels. This does not fit the patient's presentation of T-cell lymphopenia.
- **Option (A) - Complement Component C3 Deficiency:** C3 deficiency leads to impaired opsonization and lysis of encapsulated bacteria, resulting in recurrent pyogenic infections, particularly with *Neisseria* species. It does not primarily affect T-cell development or function and would not cause the observed lymphopenia or absent TRECs.
- **Option (E) - Bruton Tyrosine Kinase (BTK) Deficiency:** BTK is a cytoplasmic tyrosine kinase essential for B-cell development. Deficiency leads to X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA). Patients have absent or severely reduced B-cells and immunoglobulins but normal T-cell numbers and function. This does not fit the patient's presentation of T-cell lymphopenia and normal B-cell numbers.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deficiency in the complement component C3: Incorrect. C3 deficiency causes recurrent bacterial infections (especially encapsulated organisms) due to impaired complement-mediated opsonization and lysis. It does not affect lymphocyte development or cause T-cell lymphopenia or absent TRECs. This is a defect in the innate immune system, not adaptive lymphocyte development.
- (B) Defect in the CD40 ligand (CD40L) gene: Incorrect. CD40L deficiency causes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal T-cell numbers and function but defective B-cell class switching, leading to high IgM and low IgG/IgA/IgE. This patient has T-cell lymphopenia, ruling out CD40L deficiency.
- (D) Deficiency in the enzyme adenosine deaminase (ADA): Incorrect. ADA deficiency is an autosomal recessive form of SCID affecting T, B, and NK cells due to toxic metabolite accumulation. While it causes SCID with lymphopenia, the γc deficiency is the *most common* cause of SCID presenting with T-cell lymphopenia and normal B-cell numbers, and absent TRECs strongly point to a defect in T-cell development within the thymus, which is characteristic of γc deficiency.
- (E) Defect in the Bruton tyrosine kinase (BTK) gene: Incorrect. BTK deficiency causes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA). Patients have severely reduced or absent B-cells and immunoglobulins but normal T-cell numbers and function. This patient has normal B-cell numbers and T-cell lymphopenia, ruling out BTK deficiency.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Common Gamma Chain (γc) Deficiency (SCID-X1) | ADA Deficiency (SCID) | CD40L Deficiency (Hyper-IgM) | BTK Deficiency (XLA) | C3 Deficiency (Complement) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------------------|-----------------------|------------------------------|----------------------|---------------------------|
| Inheritance          | X-linked Recessive                          | Autosomal Recessive   | X-linked Recessive            | X-linked Recessive  | Autosomal Recessive       |
| Primary Defect       | IL-7Rα (γc) signaling                       | Purine metabolism     | T-B cell interaction         | B-cell development  | Complement activation     |
| T-cells              | Absent/Severely Reduced                     | Absent/Severely Reduced | Normal                       | Normal               | Normal                    |
| B-cells              | Present (non-functional)                    | Absent/Severely Reduced | Present (non-functional)    | Absent/Severely Reduced | Normal                    |
| NK cells             | Absent/Severely Reduced                     | Absent/Severely Reduced | Normal                       | Normal               | Normal                    |
| Immunoglobulins      | Low (all classes)                           | Low (all classes)     | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE     | Low (all classes)   | Normal (usually)          |
| TRECs                | Absent                                      | Absent                | Present                      | Present              | N/A                       |
| Typical Infections   | Severe, broad (bacterial, viral, fungal)    | Severe, broad         | Opportunistic (Pneumocystis) | Encapsulated bacteria | Encapsulated bacteria     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The most common cause of SCID presenting with T-cell lymphopenia, normal B-cell numbers, and absent TRECs is a defect in the common gamma chain (γc). Differentiating SCID from other primary immunodeficiencies like XLA (B-cell defect), Hyper-IgM syndrome (T-B cell interaction defect), and complement deficiencies requires careful analysis of lymphocyte subsets and immunoglobulin levels. Absent TRECs specifically indicate a failure in T-cell development in the thymus.

<a id='question-045'></a>

### Question 045: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 45 (months.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on the clinical presentation and underlying immunological defect associated with defects in complement components, particularly C3 deficiency, and its susceptibility to specific encapsulated bacteria. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，重点是补体成分缺陷，特别是C3缺陷，及其对特定荚膜细菌的易感性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant presents to the emergency department with fever, irritability, and signs of sepsis. He was born full-term without complications but has had recurrent sinopulmonary infections since 3 months of age, including otitis media and pneumonia. His mother reports that he has not received any vaccinations yet. Physical examination reveals a temperature of 39.5°C, tachycardia, tachypnea, and diffuse rhonchi on lung auscultation. Laboratory studies show leukocytosis with neutrophilia. A blood culture grows Gram-negative encapsulated diplococci. Gram stain of the blood culture shows characteristic lancet-shaped diplococci. Serological testing reveals markedly decreased levels of C3 and normal levels of C1q, C4, and terminal complement components (C5-C9).

Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological defect in this patient?

(A) Defect in B-cell maturation leading to agammaglobulinemia
(B) Defect in T-cell mediated immunity leading to severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID)
(C) Defect in phagocyte function leading to chronic granulomatous disease (CGD)
(D) Defect in the alternative pathway of complement activation
(E) Defect in the classical pathway of complement activation

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age (6 months):** This age is significant because maternal antibodies start waning around 3-6 months, making infants susceptible to infections if their own immune system is deficient.
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections (otitis media, pneumonia) since 3 months:** This pattern strongly suggests an underlying immunodeficiency, particularly one affecting humoral immunity or complement, as these systems are crucial for defense against encapsulated bacteria common in the respiratory tract.
- **Gram-negative encapsulated diplococci:** This morphology and Gram stain result are highly characteristic of *Neisseria meningitidis*. Encapsulated bacteria are a hallmark of infections seen in complement deficiencies.
- **Decreased C3, normal C1q, C4, C5-C9:** This specific pattern of complement component levels is crucial. Low C3 with normal levels of other components points towards a defect in the pathway that consumes C3, which is the alternative pathway. The classical pathway (C1q, C4, C2) and the terminal pathway (C5-C9) are intact, as indicated by normal levels.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Complement System Overview:** The complement system is a crucial part of innate immunity, involving a cascade of proteins that lead to opsonization (C3b), inflammation (C3a, C5a), and direct lysis of pathogens (membrane attack complex, MAC, formed by C5b-C9).
- **Complement Pathways:** There are three main pathways:
    - **Classical Pathway:** Activated by antibody-antigen complexes (IgG or IgM). Involves C1q, C4, C2.
    - **Alternative Pathway:** Activated spontaneously on microbial surfaces or by certain bacterial components (e.g., lipopolysaccharide). Involves C3, Factor B, Factor D, Properdin.
    - **Lectin Pathway:** Activated by mannose-binding lectin (MBL) binding to microbial carbohydrates. Similar to the classical pathway after activation.
- **C3 Central Role:** C3 is the central component where all three pathways converge. Activation of any pathway leads to the cleavage of C3 into C3a (anaphylatoxin) and C3b (opsonin). C3b then initiates the formation of the C5 convertase, leading to the terminal pathway and MAC formation.
- **Defects in Complement:** Deficiencies in complement components lead to increased susceptibility to infections, particularly by encapsulated bacteria like *Neisseria meningitidis*, *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, and *Haemophilus influenzae*.
- **C3 Deficiency:** Deficiency in C3 is the most common severe complement deficiency. It impairs all three pathways because C3 is essential for their convergence. Patients with C3 deficiency are highly susceptible to recurrent, severe infections with encapsulated bacteria, often presenting in infancy or early childhood. The pattern of low C3 with normal C1q, C4, and C5-C9 is diagnostic.
- **Alternative Pathway Defect:** Defects in the alternative pathway components (Factor B, Factor D, Properdin) also lead to impaired C3 activation and increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria. These defects often present similarly to C3 deficiency, although C3 levels might be normal or only slightly reduced, while C3b levels are low. However, given the markedly decreased C3 level in the vignette, a defect leading to C3 consumption (like a defect in its production or regulation) or a defect in the pathway that activates C3 (alternative pathway) is most likely. The question asks for the *underlying immunological defect*, and a defect in the alternative pathway activation is a primary cause of C3 deficiency or impaired C3 activation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defect in B-cell maturation leading to agammaglobulinemia (e.g., X-linked agammaglobulinemia, Bruton's agammaglobulinemia):** This condition involves a lack of mature B cells and antibodies (IgG, IgA, IgM). Patients typically present with recurrent bacterial infections (especially encapsulated bacteria) after 6 months of age when maternal antibodies wane. However, complement levels would be normal. The vignette specifies low C3, ruling this out.
- (B) **Defect in T-cell mediated immunity leading to severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID):** SCID involves severe defects in both T and B cell function. Patients present very early in infancy (often within the first few months) with severe, opportunistic infections (viral, fungal, bacterial), failure to thrive, and chronic diarrhea. While bacterial infections occur, the pattern and the specific complement finding (low C3) are not typical for SCID. T cell defects primarily impair cell-mediated immunity and helper function for B cells, but don't directly cause complement deficiency.
- (C) **Defect in phagocyte function leading to chronic granulomatous disease (CGD):** CGD is a defect in the NADPH oxidase enzyme complex in phagocytes, impairing their ability to kill certain catalase-positive bacteria and fungi. Patients present with recurrent abscesses (skin, liver, lung) and granuloma formation caused by organisms like *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, and *Aspergillus*. Complement levels are normal in CGD. The vignette describes infections with encapsulated bacteria and low C3, inconsistent with CGD.
- (E) **Defect in the classical pathway of complement activation (e.g., C1q, C4, C2 deficiency):** Deficiencies in classical pathway components increase susceptibility to infections with encapsulated bacteria, particularly *Streptococcus pneumoniae* and *Haemophilus influenzae*, and are associated with autoimmune diseases like systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE). However, in classical pathway deficiency, C3 levels are typically normal because the alternative pathway can still activate C3. The vignette specifically states low C3 levels, making a primary classical pathway defect less likely than a defect affecting C3 itself or the alternative pathway which activates C3.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | C3 Deficiency / Alternative Pathway Defect | Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) | SCID                     | CGD                                  | Classical Pathway Defect |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :----------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | C3 production/regulation or Alt. pathway components | B-cell maturation       | T-cell development      | Phagocyte NADPH oxidase              | Classical pathway components |
| **Complement Levels**   | Low C3, Normal C1q, C4, C5-C9              | Normal                  | Normal                  | Normal                               | Normal C3, Low C1q/C4/C2 |
| **Antibody Levels**     | Normal                                     | Very Low/Absent          | Low (T-cell help needed) | Normal                               | Normal                  |
| **Cellular Immunity**   | Normal                                     | Normal                  | Severely Impaired       | Normal                               | Normal                  |
| **Phagocyte Function**  | Normal                                     | Normal                  | Normal                  | Impaired (killing catalase+ organisms) | Normal                  |
| **Typical Infections**  | Encapsulated bacteria (*Neisseria*, *S. pneumoniae*, *H. influenzae*) | Encapsulated bacteria | Opportunistic (viral, fungal, bacterial) | Catalase+ bacteria, fungi | Encapsulated bacteria, SLE |
| **Age of Onset**        | Infancy/Childhood                          | >6 months                | Early infancy           | Childhood                             | Variable                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria in infancy, coupled with low C3 levels and normal levels of other complement components, is highly suggestive of a defect in the alternative pathway of complement activation or a deficiency in C3 itself. This contrasts with primary antibody deficiencies (normal complement), T-cell deficiencies (SCID, often opportunistic infections), phagocyte defects (CGD, specific organisms/abscesses), and classical pathway deficiencies (normal C3). Remember the central role of C3 and the pattern of complement levels in diagnosing specific deficiencies.

<a id='question-046'></a>

### Question 46: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 1 Focus 46 (highlights)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in T-cell development and function, particularly DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome), and its associated clinical manifestations and immunological findings. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是T细胞发育和功能缺陷，例如DiGeorge综合征（22号染色体11.2区缺失），以及相关的临床表现和免疫学发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 10-month-old male infant presents to the pediatrician with recurrent episodes of oral thrush (candidiasis) and pneumonia. His mother reports that he has difficulty feeding and often chokes. Physical examination reveals a small, upturned nose, low-set ears, hypertelorism, and a prominent forehead. Cardiac auscultation reveals a heart murmur. Laboratory investigations show lymphopenia (absolute lymphocyte count of 500/µL) with a markedly reduced number of T lymphocytes (CD3+ cells < 100/µL), while B lymphocyte (CD19+ cells) and NK cell (CD16+/CD56+ cells) counts are relatively normal. Immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM) are low. A genetic test confirms a heterozygous deletion on chromosome 22q11.2.

Which of the following immunological defects is most likely responsible for this patient's recurrent infections?

(A) Defective B-cell maturation leading to agammaglobulinemia.
(B) Impaired complement activation due to C3 deficiency.
(C) Deficient T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling pathway.
(D) Abnormal development of the thymus gland.
(E) Defective phagocyte function due to chronic granulomatous disease (CGD).

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 10-month-old male infant. Age is relevant for presentation of primary immunodeficiencies.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Recurrent oral thrush (Candida albicans infection) and pneumonia (suggestive of T-cell mediated immunity defect, as T-cells are crucial for controlling intracellular pathogens like fungi and certain bacteria/viruses). Difficulty feeding and choking can be related to anatomical abnormalities.
- **Physical Examination:** Characteristic facial features (small nose, low-set ears, hypertelorism, prominent forehead) and heart murmur are classic signs of DiGeorge syndrome.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Lymphopenia, specifically severe reduction in T lymphocytes (CD3+ cells), while B cells (CD19+) and NK cells (CD16+/CD56+) are relatively normal. Low immunoglobulin levels are secondary to lack of T-cell help for B-cell maturation and antibody production.
- **Genetic Test:** Confirms a heterozygous deletion on chromosome 22q11.2, diagnostic for DiGeorge syndrome (also known as 22q11.2 deletion syndrome).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying immunological defect responsible for the recurrent infections.

The combination of characteristic facial features, cardiac murmur, recurrent opportunistic infections (thrush, pneumonia), lymphopenia with T-cell deficiency, and the confirmed 22q11.2 deletion strongly points to DiGeorge syndrome. The core immunological defect in DiGeorge syndrome is related to the abnormal development of the third and fourth pharyngeal pouches, which give rise to the thymus and parathyroid glands.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **DiGeorge Syndrome Pathophysiology:** The TBX1 gene, located on chromosome 22q11.2, is crucial for the development of the third and fourth pharyngeal pouches during embryogenesis. Deletion of this region leads to hypoplasia or aplasia of the thymus and parathyroid glands.
- **Thymus Development:** The thymus is the primary site for T-cell maturation. T-cell precursors migrate from the bone marrow to the thymus, where they undergo positive and negative selection to become mature, immunocompetent T cells (CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells).
- **Immunological Consequences:** In DiGeorge syndrome, the underdeveloped thymus cannot effectively produce mature T cells, leading to severe T-cell deficiency (T-cell lymphopenia). This deficiency impairs cell-mediated immunity, making patients susceptible to opportunistic infections caused by intracellular pathogens like Candida, Pneumocystis jirovecii, viruses, and certain bacteria. B-cell function is also affected because T-cell help (via CD40L-CD40 interaction and cytokine secretion) is required for optimal B-cell activation, class switching, and memory B-cell formation, leading to hypogammaglobulinemia. NK cell function is typically normal.
- **Kaplan Review & First Aid Correlation:** Kaplan Preclinical Immunology and Microbiology Review 2023 (pp. 118–128) and First Aid 2025 (Immunology: Primary Immunodeficiency Diseases) describe DiGeorge syndrome as a primary immunodeficiency caused by a 22q11.2 deletion, resulting in thymic hypoplasia/aplasia, T-cell deficiency, and associated clinical features (cardiac defects, facial dysmorphism, hypocalcemia).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Defective B-cell maturation leading to agammaglobulinemia. This describes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), caused by mutations in the BTK gene. Patients with XLA have normal T-cell numbers and function but lack mature B cells and antibodies. This patient has low T cells and relatively normal B cells (though function is impaired secondarily), making XLA incorrect.
- (B) Impaired complement activation due to C3 deficiency. C3 deficiency is a primary immunodeficiency affecting the complement system. Patients are susceptible to recurrent pyogenic bacterial infections, particularly encapsulated organisms. While infections occur, the specific pattern (opportunistic, fungal, viral) and the T-cell deficiency are not characteristic of C3 deficiency.
- (C) Deficient T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling pathway. Defects in TCR signaling (e.g., ZAP-70 deficiency) lead to impaired T-cell activation and function. While this causes T-cell deficiency and susceptibility to infections, it doesn't explain the thymic hypoplasia, facial features, or cardiac defects seen in this patient. The primary defect in DiGeorge is thymic development, not the TCR signaling itself.
- (E) Defective phagocyte function due to chronic granulomatous disease (CGD). CGD is caused by defects in the NADPH oxidase enzyme complex in phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages). Patients are susceptible to recurrent bacterial and fungal infections that form granulomas (e.g., Staphylococcus aureus, Aspergillus). This patient's primary defect is T-cell deficiency, not phagocyte function.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                  | DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 deletion) | XLA (BTK mutation) | C3 Deficiency | TCR Signaling Defect (e.g., ZAP-70) | CGD (NADPH Oxidase Defect) |
| :----------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :---------------- | :------------ | :--------------------------------- | :------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**       | Thymic hypoplasia/aplasia            | B-cell maturation | Complement    | TCR signaling                      | Phagocyte oxidative burst  |
| **Affected Cells**       | T cells (primarily)                  | B cells           | Complement    | T cells                            | Phagocytes                 |
| **Key Lab Finding**      | T-cell lymphopenia                   | Absent B cells    | Low C3        | Impaired T-cell activation         | Defective ROS production   |
| **Typical Infections**   | Opportunistic (Candida, Pneumocystis) | Encapsulated bacteria | Pyogenic bacteria | Opportunistic (similar to DiGeorge) | Staph, Aspergillus, Serratia |
| **Other Features**       | Cardiac defects, facial dysmorphism, hypocalcemia | Normal T cells | Normal T/B cells | Normal T/B cell numbers           | Granuloma formation        |
| **Chromosome/Gene**      | 22q11.2 deletion (TBX1)              | Xq21.3 (BTK)      | Various       | Various                            | Various                    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
DiGeorge syndrome is a primary immunodeficiency characterized by thymic hypoplasia/aplasia due to a 22q11.2 deletion, leading to T-cell deficiency and susceptibility to opportunistic infections. Key features include characteristic facial dysmorphism, cardiac defects, and hypocalcemia. Differentiate it from other primary immunodeficiencies like XLA (B-cell defect), CVID (variable B and T cell defects), CGD (phagocyte defect), and complement deficiencies based on the specific cell lineage affected and the pattern of infections.
